The Transparent Scarf.—With the warm days, will come the transparent scarf, which will take the place of the now indispensahle feather or marabout boa. The new scarfs are all immensely wide, and built of such fabrics as silk muslin, chiffon, gauze, or crepe de chine. They are handsomely embroidered at the ends, and heavily fringed, or weighted with tassels. These beauti- ful and becoming little wraps are greatly in evidence for evening wear, at theatres and restaurants, and later on will make a charming addition to airy-fairy summer toilettes. May-day Superstitions.—Among many old beliefs and _

customs was the idea that May-dew was an exisellent thing for the cnmplexion. So it is, undoubtedly, but'i its chief virtue lies not so much in the dew_ itself, as in I _

the fact that one must get up early to catch it. 'That, the scientists tells us, is the only merit which May-clew ; has over the dew of December, but it is a very old superstition that May-day dew was a most efficacious beautifier. It can at any rate be easily tried by steeping some soft linen in the glistening drops on the grass, using only that for a face wash. Most of the old May- day customs and superstitions however have ,lied out. One to which the damsels of former times attached much importance was the divination afforded by the early morning snail-track and the letters thus formed

1 By subjecting minerals containing various metals to the 'spark of a high-tension induc- tion coil, characteristic colours are, says the !, Engineer, imparted to the spark by the differ- ent metals. it is not the heat that produces these colours, 'but centrifugal • force, which, like radio-active matter, puts the corpuscles ! of the molecule in motion.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

A Family of Courtiers.—Miss Beatrice Knollys, who is to marry Sir Cape! Charles Wolseley on May 9th, is a niece of Miss Charlotte Knollys, Queen Alexandra's most intimate friend and confidante. Miss Charlotte Knollys has been Lady-in-Waiting to the Queen since , her Majesty's marriage in 1863; and is known to all members of the Royal Family as" Chatty." There are no affairs, however much of a private or family nature, , upon which the queen does not confide in Miss Knollys, who for forty-four years has been invaluable to her I Majesty on account of her knowledge of foreign I languages, her tact, and capability in discharging secre- tarial duties. Her brother, Lord Knollys, has been almost as long in the King's service, and has been his Majesty's friend and private secretary for nearly forty years. For centuries the Knollys family has been intimately associated with the Court, and this connec- tion is likely to be continued. Lord and Lady Knollys' only son is a page of honour to the King, and their only daughter Miss Lavinia Knollys, is expected to be appointed Maid of Honour when the next vacancy arises. Wedding Rings.—There has been much talk about the elaborate wedding ring comirig into favour put although now and again a Society bride is found to follow this new departure, it is safe to say the idea will never become popular. Mottoes on the other hand are becom- ing more and more general, and invariably nowadays find a place inside the wedding ring. The custom of wearing the wedding ring on the third finger of the left band goes back for its origin to the Egyptians from whom the Greeks borrowed the custom, and handed it over to the Romans. The third finger was dedicated to Apollo, the sun god, and gold was an additional symbol of the sun. In Germany the engagement ring frequently serves for the wedding ring, also, being worn on the right hand until after marriage when it is transferred to the left. In Spain, the engage- ment ring is most ornate, and is invariably worn on the third finger of the right hand. Our Colonial Visitors.—The Colonial ladies, now on a visit to this country, afford ample proof that the art of dressing is well understood in-distant parts of the Empire. Naturally they are all greatly interested in the London shops, but the most charming" creations" have not apparently tempted them to depart from the standard of neatness and good taste, which invariably Marks their style of attire. Miss Botha, the daughter of the famous General, has an exquisite complexion, with beautiful fair hair, and altogether presents a charming appearance, which makes her one of the most observed of our Colonial visitors., Miss Lyne, whose father is the Premier of New South Wales, is the only one of the Colonial party who has never been in this country, before. Mrs. Deakin, the wife of the Premier of the Australian Commonwealth, was here seven years ago, and among other chsnges, she says, that strike her is the great elaboration of women's dress of the present day. That we have gone to extremes in sumptuous and extravagant dress is quite true, and the staart and fashionable women who dress to be "priced," and to out-vie one another have, unfortunately, their imitators more or less among all classes. Hair Ornaments.—The stalls of a fashionable thaatre afford an education in the evening, in the matter of head ornamentation. At the moment we appear to be letting our imaginations wander towards Egypt, and are tiring our beans in a style which recalls the great Cleopatra. There are, for example, the bands of curious jewels, set in rough silver, huge ropes of seed pearls, solid-looking wreaths of leaves, flat bands of diamonds—worn across the front of the head— and tassels. For the latter, seed pearls and diamonds, are usually employed. Taken altogether, the present mode is an improvement upon the weird feather stage, through which we have just passed, when the notions exhibited might well have been culled from a tribe of wild Indians. _ A Becoming Mode.—The black velvet neck-band, threaded through paste or diamond slides, is in con- siderable request for evening wear, even among those women who possess jewelled collars. It has been said that the black velvet neck band is the most becoming neck ornament a woman can have, and undoubtedly there is much of truth in this statement. Those of us who number among ouç stores some beautiful old slides are fortunate indeed, but the girl or woman who has to depend upon latter day specimens need not despair. Many excellent and inexpensive copies of exquisite old- world examples, are to be fousd in the shops now-a-days and these look their best when mounted on black velvet.

===== 0002601_19070427_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW FASMON,

The latest novelties shown this month, in Paris, Which promise to .become a craze as the season Advances, are little boleros of Chantilly lace worn with gowns of bright-colonred crepe-de- Chine or mousseline de sole. These, says Lon- ddn Opinion, are made of black , and are very light and effective, their picturesquenes heightened by a large black picture hat, which helps to subdue the .brii,lt-hued costumes the milliners of Paris are trying. to introduce, which, if they catch the pu.blic ta,qe, will call for black accessories, such tis scarves, hats. and collars, that will soften and 'tone down the somewhat startling effect of vivid greens. bright violets, and brilliant shades of ro,se colour.

After it few days came the gratifying answer that the Duchess Was quite willing to become the Patroness of 'The .White Ribbon.'" HAT FOR TRAVELLING. Quite unusually smart and becoming is the inieat little travelling or knockabout hat which is the subject of our sketch. The hat itself sits closely to the head, and is of a modified—very much modified—boat shape, the straw of 'which it is made being thick, strong, and of a pretty biscuit shade. Over brim and crown in front are twist4d bands of brown and white ribbon, the ends being tied into a smart bow which rests upon the crown. On either aide of the hat, about half-way between front and back, are arranged two beautiful soft wings of mingled brown, fawn,

MADGE.

and white feathers. These" wings completely hide the back of the hat, meeting behind and droop- ing on to the hair beneath. The same idea might be successfully carried out in a black and white, blue and white, or purple and white colour scheme.

ON LINGERIE.

Society women spend thought . and much money on their lingerie, and on what the French call their beaux dessous generally, writes "A 'Society Butterfly" in if. .A pretty idea is to choose a favourite shade of colour—say pink or blue—and allow this to pervade the whole of one's underwear. Black and pink or, white and pink corsets, pink ribbons for bows and shoulder- straps, pink garters, and a pink petticoat—such a scheme is charming. And pink ribbons would adorn the night-attire, and -the cant-dc lit and dressing-gown might also match in colouring. Some women go "co far as to have knots of the same ribbon tied on their lace-trimmed pillow- slips, and their guipure coverlets are lined to correspond. Camisoles—called by our mothers "petticoat-bodices "—are , built of finest - cambric. trimmed with yalenciennes lace and much of what emperts describe as "veining." Corsets fix' the clot: of a costume, and are, in fact, its foundation Silk coutil, unlined, is used by the best stay-makers, bones are small and slender, and as a result the corsets take small space under- the bodice of one's dress, and are smart, light, and comfortable. Straight-fronted corsets still prevail, and, in spite of statements to the con- trary, tight-lacing. is seldom or never adopted by women in smart society. TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

VEAL CUTLETS A LA SAINT GERNIAIN.—Tako 11b. of veal cutlets, some freshly-boiled green peas, six small mushrooms. chopped, two shallots, chopped, 20z. of fresh butter, one dessertspoon- ful of finely=chopped parsley, one and a-half gills of good white sauce, the yolks of two eggs, a little lemon juice, find some finely-smashed- potato. Trim tile, cutlets into neat rounds all of the same size. Mat the frying-pan, • and when hot put in the cutlets and the chopped shallots and mush- rooms, and fry the cutlets both sides for a few minutes, being careful 'not to let them brown. Then drain off the butter, put the cutlets, shal- lots,' mushrooms, and white sauce into a stew- pan, and let all saute gently over the fire for about a; quarter of an hour until the cutlets are tender. Have the mashed potato arranged in a firm circle on a hot dish, and arrange the cut- lets neatly on the mashed potato and nut them aside to iteep nor. 416 Cl tne neaten yolks or tho 'eggs' to the sauce in the stewpan and stir over the fire until it thickens. Then add, the chopped parsley and the lemon juice, and pour this sauce over and around the cutlets. Fill in the centre of the circle with freshly-boiled young peas.

POISONED BY OZONE,

Serious stomach .troubles among workmen in large electric plants are attributed by a German authority to •ozone-poisoning .and not to electric radiations. Ozone—which is excel- lent in a reasonable dosage—is . produced in large quantities, and with atmospheric nitro- gen it forms nitric acid, explaining the acid taste often noticed. An effective remedy is free ventilation and separation of high- tension apparatus from workrooms.

THE DETECTION OF GOLD IN SANDS.

The. Compte.Q Rendus has published a pro- cess for the analysis of auriferous sands by A. Fournier. From 5 to 10 per cent. of man- ganese dioxide is added to the sand, and the mixture is treated in the cold with hydro- chloric acid for twenty-four hours, and then on the water bath until the liquid becomes quite clear. After decanting from siliceou4 matter the solution is evaporated to a syrupy consistence and added to a large amount of alcohol. Sulphuretted hydrogen in excess is passed into the warm alcoholic solution. The precipitated sulphides are washed with water slightly acidified with hydrochloric acid, then treated with . the warm acid, and the final re- sidue, whiclk contains the noble metals dis- solved in hydrochloric acid containing a little nitric acid. The solution thus obtained is evaporated to dryness, and the residue taken up with hydrochloric acid and water. The gold and other allied metals are then de- posited from this solution by one, of the ordi- nary precipitation processes, (ammonium chloride, oxalic acid, &c.).

THE GRINDELWALD GLACIERS.

Men of science were first attracted to Switzerland by the Grindelwald Glaciers; they were called "Miracles of Nature." These glaciers have been described • as two great tongues of ice, which thrust themselves down among the meadows and corn and cot- tages. It is only some fifty years ago that the Alpine Club was founded in this country. Many of the nations of Western Europe have since formed similar clubs, and consequently the scientific as well as the geographical ex-, ploration of the mountains has been carried out. It has been found that, whereas it was first believed that glaciers meet •in winter, the phenomenon is caused by a spring under the glacier. An , international commission on glaciers was founded, and a French commis- sion has also been studying in detail the glaciers in the French Alps. There have been men with scientific minds who -have spent much time in attempting to connect glaciers with sunspots. It i 8 known that they are con- nected with the snow and rain which fell in past seasons, but it is a difficult matter to establish absolute proof that .these are con- nected with the spots on the sun.

===== 0002601_19070427_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
STARS FROM A WELL.

There has existed a very ancient belief, going back to the time of the Greeks and the Romans, that overhead stars can be seen in daylight from the bottom of wells or pits. Arisotle and Pliny believed in the possibility cf this being done, and so apparently did Sir John .Herschel. In his treatise on astronomy, Herschel writes: "From the bottom of deep, narrow pits, such as a well or the shaft of", a mine' such bright stars as pass the zenith may be discerned by the naked eye. We have ourselves heard it stated by a celebrated optician that the earliest circumstance which drew his attention to astronomy was the' regular appearance, at a certain hour for seve•. ral successive days, of a considerable star through the shaft or a chimney." It is a pity that Herschel did not give the name of his "celebrated optician," for the

POSSIBILITY OF THUS SEEING STAES is called in question. Mr. Maunder, of the Greenwich Observatory, writing in Know- ledge, doubts if there is any authenticated instance of it. Humboldt repeatedly tried the experiment.' in Siberian and American mines, mid not Only -failed himself ever to detect a star, but never met anyone who had succeeded. More recently an American astro- nomer set Up a tube for the express purpose of seeing the Pleiades by daylight, also with- out effect. The assistance of a telescope enables the brighter stars ,and planets to be discerned at mid-day, but a dark well or pit is not exactly a telescope, and the conditions are different. Perhaps, therefore, this vener- able tradition may have to go the way of many others.

AN UNSUSPECTED ELECTRIC FISH.

-America is the home of certain remarkable fishes known as star-gazers (Astroscopus), from the, fact that the eyes are placed near the middle .of the large, flat head in such a position that the line of vision is directed almost immediately upwards. In correlation with this peculiarity is the upward direction of the large and strongly-armed moulh, which is likewise placed entirely on the upper sur- face of the head. Obviously such a fish can- not obtain its prey in the ordinary manner, and its mode of feeding therefore needs ex- planation. According to Messrs. Dahlgren and Silvester, of .Princeton University, the explanation is possibly to be found in the fact that the heads of these fishes are furnished with an electrical organ; which is of a type quite different 'froth that of any other electric fish. The power possessed by the star-gazers of emitting electric shocks appears to have been known from time., immemorial to the Virginian fisherman, but it is only lately, and then by an accident, that it became known to men of. science. It is now suggested that these fishes ,may • kill their prey by electric dis- charges, and then catch them as they fall in the strangely upturned mouth. Some support to this theory is afforded by the fact that the stomach of the one specimen dissected con- tained a number of small swift-swimming fishes.

THE EXPLOITS OF THE ONE-EYED MAN.

BY EDWARD TOWLER.

PROLOGUE.

The Britannic drew alongside the Prince's landing-stage at. Liverpool, and the One-eyed Man stepped briskly, along the gangway to the shore. The voyage was . ended, and he was glad ; but as he whistled softly to himself the thought ran through his mind that possibly to others his arrival in England would not be so welcome. His face was lean and dark, and .a pair of bushy eyebrows. nearly meeting at the junction of the nose, gave a sinister look to a countenance that did not require the addetT detail of a missing eye to accentuate its evil, aspect. It was a face 'that made people shudder involuntarily, and if he had lived in the Middle Ages he would probably haVe made an early, acquaintance with the stake for being posSessed of an evil eye. His thin lips were compressed tightly, although at the corners There lurked what might be fitly de- scribed as the ghost of Is wintry smile, for he never smiled :openly. His dress was a quiet Scotch tweed, grey in colour, and he carried a portmanteau in his hand, and a rug over his arm. Stich was the personality of the man who was destined to make England ring with his _exploits. , _ _ _

lit had lipoked - under the name of Johna- than, P. Brown, of Jersey City, U.S.A., and had an intermediate ticket from, I\l",,ew York to Liverpool. On landing he went straight to the General Post Office and inquired for letters or telegrams. He was handed a tele- gram. It was addressed to James P. Smyth, of Pittsburg, and from this it will be gathered that the One:eyed Man was particular neither as to his name nor his address. He tore the envelope open and read: " Dear father, come home at once; James very bad. No "hope,. Given up." Again the wintry smile played about. th thin, hard mouth, and the one eye gleamed with cat-like brilliancy • then he went straight to the: Lime-street Railway Station, and night found him at the door of a house off the Euston-road, in London: He hesitated a moment before knocking; indeed, upon second thoughts,- he drew a latchkey from his pocket and. "quietly let himself in. • For a moment he stood in the hall, then silently stepped up to the ,closed dOO% of a moth, on the left side of a passage, and listened. "I tell you," said a voice, "I care no more for your One-eyed Man than I do for that pot dog on the mantelpiece." A second vOice said, "I'm with you .there. 'There's a lot too much fuss made over him. We do the work, he collars the dollars, rot him!"

"I say," said the first, " what's his name— his real- name, I mean? He's aliases by the score, but hang me if 1., know who he is, al- though 1,. as it were, work for him." .

• "Cali him anything. Down in Jersey City he's known as Captain Nick. 'Frisco knows him as the One-eyed Boss, and to you and me he's known as No. 1," said the second.

"Say." said the first, "what became of the , balance of that haul he made on the Grand Trunk Railway last fall? There was a .hundred thousand dollars, , but only fifty thousand Whacked out."

"There you have me," answered the other,

"Well, I've had enough. I shall split, and you and 1,. know enough to get him a lifer. There's twenty thousand dollars reward for him in the States. That's ten thousand apiece —what do you say?"

"He's a terror to, handle, I admit," said the other: "But, hang it! we're not kid& I'm with you. We'll blow the gaff on him to- morrow." " Where is he now ? " asked the first.

'The door opened, and the One-eyed Man stepped in. "He is here," he said, quietly. , The two men, seated one on each side of the fire, sprang to their feet, then dropped back .speechless.

The One-eyed Man ,took a chair between them and ' the. door, surveyed tfiem silently for a few minutes, and then said, "I knew you Were about to 'give the show -away, James Thornton, but as for you, Richard Grant, well, I confess I have been deceived in you. Do you know what you • have incurred? Fools !". he went on, fiercely, his eye blazing with passion. " Fools, to pit your addled brains against mine ! Did you think I should not tumble to your 'game? If you are false, there are men in my League true as steel, and, though you did not think it, even though I am -Thousands of miles away, eyes watch on my account, and, by Heaven, my one eye is worth a hundred of your four, you blind rats ! "

The two men cowered in their chairs.

"You are two Men," he went on, sneer- ingly; "you said so only just now. , I even think Mr. Grant went so far as to admit you were -not kids,' if I caught the words aright th•ne,ugli the door, -did you not,/ Mr. Grant?" -Grant did not speak, but his grey face told of mortal terror.

"Never mind," went on the other, "silence gives consent. And you, James Thornton, what of your catechism of my conduct? A hundred thousand dollars from the bullion car and only fifty thousand whacked out, as you so vulgarly term it. Well, do we not pay the master mind fifty per cent.? Do I not plan these schemes.? Is it not my brain which conceives them? Does the private soldier get the pay of the General in command? No. Well, no more do you. You are but privates, and poor ones at that, but you shall be paid. Yes, by Heaven you shall, and this time yea will net grumble at your reward, for your wages shall -be paid in full to-night, now, once for all. • Men who threaten, me do not live to carry out that threat. Do you remem- ber Sam Hankey? He thought as you did. ! I see you do. Well, that should have given you food for reflection before making your rash resolves." ,

The man " addressed as James Thornton essavr.d to speak, but his tongue, clove to his mouth, and when he did manage to stutter out a sound it re2olved itself in the one word "Mercy." Before the man Grant could utter a protest. the One-eyed Man whipped peculiar - locking pistol from his pocket, pointed it at Thornton, pressed' the trigger, and the man fell dead. There was no smoke, no noise it waS an absolutely silent death. The murderer turned to • Grant, who had watched, horror-struck.

" What dPvilry is this? Is he dead? No, he be," he cried. "He is, unfortunately. Mr. Grant: but there is no devilry. This pistol is simply an ingenious invention of my own. It is on the pneumatic isystem of compressed air, so to speak, and has the advantage, as- you have just seen', 'of being absolutely silent." "You will not shoot me? .Spare me wailed Grant in an agony of terror, but he might have snoken to the wall, for almest un- noticed; so diabolical was ,the mechanism of the weapon, the One-eyed Man again fired, and Grant spun round and fell in' a heap be- side the body of the other man.

The One-eyed Man went throve:li their pockets silently and swiftly, trAing only papers and letters, so as to leave no clue to their identity. . Then, goinir, to the mirror, he took something from a small box in his pocket, and inserted it in his em»fy eye-socket. When he turned from the glass he was no longer the One-eyed Man. He had noiv two eyes: and it would have taken a clever detective to know him for the same man. Then he put on a moustache with sow spirit Ll' , 1111 a silently left the house with .its dreadful secret.

An hour brought him to another house, on .the borders of Notting-hill. This time. he knocked, and the door ~vas opened by a man who greeted him cordially. I.—THE EAST ANGLIAN BANK ROBBERY. The scene of the events here related was the east coast of England. In a small, straggling village not more than six miles from a railway centre, the occupants of a small room, two in number, were conning a cyclist's road map of the district. They were the One-eyed Man and his lieutenant, who went by the name of " Greaser " among the gang, from some trace of Mexican blood in him; but in the village he was known by the more euphonious name of Rowlands T. Rowlandi, The -One-eyed Man's cognomen was Henry Macassdr- They had been 'lodging • in Shering- .

ham for four t-veeks, ostensibly fOt health arid pleasure. With little excursions, fishing on the Broads, and other mild pursuits, such as sketching, they whiled away the daylight hours, whilst night saw them in the sanctity .of their bedroom scanning, by the aid of an oil 'lamp, the contour of the coast, and the roads therefrom. On the night in question they were com- pleting the details of their plan, and , were poring over the map, when the One-eyed Man spoke abruptly. "l-Te'e's the spot," he said, pointing with his finger to • a dot on the coast. His lieutenant, leaning over his left shoul- der, read " Cley." ! "That's it," continued the One-eyed Man.; ; "nice and quiet, and a good road through to Holt and Fakenham, and so on to Peterboro'. Good road to double back on Norwich or : through to London.. Are the tyres all right?" "Right as the mail," said the other. "Well, . to-morrow night the Submarine , will lie off Cloy, and we shall ship on her the proceeds of our robbery of the East Anglian Bank in Norwich easily in an hour, barring ! accident. to the Panhard. I fix the time for beginning operations at 3.30 p.m." "Great snakes! - Broad daylight ! " almost yelled the lieutenant. The One-eyed Man looked coldly at him. ," I'm afraid, Greaser, you have got to learn that my method of procedure differs somewhat from that of the common or garden indivi- dual who prowls round with a dark lantern and a jemmy." , • "Oh, I knew you -were a clinker at the game, Boss; but' whoever cracks a crib then? And a bank at that! Why, the clerks will be there!" "They will not, for the bank shuts at three, and most of them go almost upon the stroke. But, granted one or two are left, they will be your especial charge, Mr. Greaser." "I suppose, Boss, you do not mind 'inform- ing me how you purpose proceeding; I am thick-headed enough to be totally in the dark." The ,One-eyed Man, smiled through his thin lips almost pityingly at the Greaser, who sat opposite to him. You poor fool," he .said—" you poor fool! ,I doubt if I shall ever make a master of you. And yet I had hopes" —and again he smiled upon him. "Do you tnk I am going to throw La dynamite bomb on the bank, or what ? " "I tell you I don't know," growled the other suddenly, with red temper showing dully on his cheek—an indication the One-eyed Man noted, for a quarrel with the Greaser on the eve of a big haul was not to be thought of. He hastened to conciliate him. "Listen, my friend," he said. "I have here ,a list of the names of the leading directors of the bank, and the first and fdre- most is that of its chairman, Lord Amberstone. To-morrow afternoon, about two o'clock, go to the manager and deliver a note, asking him to accompany you to the Maid's Head Hotel at 2.30 to Meet the chairman upon im- portant business. He is a new .manager. and does not know Lord Amberstone personally. I rely Upon that for the success of my scheMe. You will conduct 'him te me in my private room, and, still in the character of my ser- vant, you will await my order. The Sub- marine has instructions to be off Cley at 7 p.m. The skipper—Bresci, the Italian—will land in a patent collapsible boat, and meet us a little to, the westward of the Roeket. Tower on the I beach. He will hide the boat in the scrubby grass that lies alongside and wait for us at the back of the huge sea-wall of -stones. We meet -him with the stuff in our motor, stow it aboard, and there you are 1" I"You forget the coastguard," said the Greaser. I do not," • said the Onereyed Man, sharply. "I ask You, am I in . the habit of ' forgetting things like that? He does not go to his rounds till nearly eight o'clock. Haven't we been moth-hunting in that beastly marsh for weeks only to ascertain the time of his rounds? We take the motor down'. the toad to the Rocket Station, and there you are ! " "Well, the plan should answer," said the Greaser in reply. "Any way, I'm on. 'Now let's Igo to bed.- By the way, what do you expect I to get in the way of boodle?" "Well, I reckon on twenty thousand I pounds in hard cash. Is that decent enough, Mr. Greaser?" The Greaser did not answer, but smiled Ihugely. . The Smart Capture at Croft.

At the Liverpool Quarter Sessions on Tuesday, for breaking into the warehouse of George "Lawson, at Southworth-with-Croft, on the 12th inst., and stealing a quantity of. brass and other articles, John Taylor, 28, and Samuel-Warburton, labourers, were sent to gaol for three months. November—and looked the aristocrat from the crown of his fashionable motor-cap to the soles of his American boots. His lieutenant, the Greaser, rang the bell, but his arrival ,haci been noted, for before nis hand was . away from the button the obsequious manager was smiling at the door. I" Tins way, my lord," he said, " this way," and the bogus Lord Amberstone stepped in, following by the lieutenant, carrying the - two: cases.

Arrived at the strong-room, the manager swung open the massive door. At the same moment the Greaser dropped the boxes, and seized the unsuspecting manager from behind. The One-eyed Man .promptly clapped a chloroform „sponge against his mouth and nose. There was a convulsive struggle; but the Greaser had a grip of iron, and held the manager like a vice for a moment or two, when his struggles ceased.

I "Let go.:' said the One-eyed Man, and the inanimate form of the ' manager was laid in- side the room with another pad over his mouth for safety. . " Quick. now Greaser ' Slip two or three boxes, in the cases." The :sham jewel-boxes e opened, and the Greaser transferred two of the bank cash- boxes, filled with coin of the realm, into one, while the One-eyed Man rapidly transferred a couple more to the other. Then, staggering under the weight, the Greaser started for daylight and' the ear, while the One-eyed Man kept watch and ward. The Greaser had reached the passage leading to the outside door when the porter came in sight. "Is your " were the only words the latter spoke, for the Greaser, like his master, did not stop at trifles, neither did he leave anything to chance. • The each was dropped upon the floor, and silently that diabolical air pistol did its work. Without even a groan, the wretched man sank upon the floor. The Greaser callously pushed the body on one side, and, picking up the box, continued his interrupted course,. deposited his cash in the secret chamber of the ear, - and returned.

wer

" Anything to report?" said the One-eyed Man.

"Met the porter, Boss." " Well ? "

" Dropped him as per instructions." " Good ! Take these now, and I will bring two more. Thirty thousand in cash is not bad."

The Greas.er did as he was bid, and then the One-eyed Man shut the doors of the strong-room vaults, and the locks snapped with a vicious click. The Greaser stopped.

Boss," he said—" Boss !" " Well? "

"Good Heavens! Boss, there's that poor beggar inside. When he comes round, he'll die like a rat in a trap."

The One-eyed Man's voice was metallic in 3 hardness.

"Greaser," he said, "be pleased to allow me a free hand. My plans, my deals, My work are the products of my 'brain, and I Now

allow no man to silence !" ," But, Boss-"

question

me.

"Shut up!" hissed the One-eyed Man. "Isn't thirty thou' good enough for you? And you've got the porter on your own shoulders."

The Greaser said no more. 3Vithout the slightest obstruction, and unquestioned by a soul, they started the motor-car out of the bank yard and turned into, the main street of the city, then, making for tke Aylshain-road,' disappeared in the fog of the dull November' afternoon. The whole thing had only taken fifteen minutes to carry out, and the clock at the Guildhall shewed only 3.40 as they slipped by in the car. It was ex.actly an hour and ten minutes afterwards that the Rocket Station at Salhouse beach came into sight. and a rural policeman's heart beat with joy for between Cromer and Sheringham they were booked for exceeding the speed limit; but, needless to. add. the Cromer Bench were short .of a de- fendant when the case was eventually called. Salthouse was deserted, an as the ear with its lights out slipped down the roadway over the marsh. to the sea, it was as a place of the dead. As they reached the station the car was brought to a standstill, and a man sprang Up. gave a signal that the Boss answered, and then the boxes were carried into the Berthon boat and transhipped to the Submarine. The One-eyed Man and the Greaser went aboard, and two members of the crew, totally different in build and dress, took their places in the motor and drove it back to London. Mean- while, the Submarine disappeared under the waves and sailed for her secret moOring- place. The next morning, not Norfolk alone, but the whole of England rang with the news of the crime; but to the perpetrators there was absolutely no clue. But the huge haul by no4means satisfied the cupidity of the One-eyed Man. Nor would his vaulting ambition permit him to rest content with his ill-gotten but ample gains. He craved' for notoriety, no matter how unenviable, and so, long before the British public and the police had ceased to wonder at the audacity of the Norwich Bank Robbery, he sent a thrill of horror through the country by an even more astounding exploit.

Welcome at any boor of the day.

in her books tragedy seems certainly' to predomin- ate. And this, I cannot but think,c is a sign of I her greatness. She *does not • please the taste of the superficial reader who likes a book in which the characters are those of the child's fairy tale, and "live happily ever afterwards." She draws from life, and must perforce draw as she sees it. She certainly does not see that life is an unmixed blessina. It has some terribly bitter aspects and Mrs. Ward (roes not hesitate to picture them in.all their native hideousness. Her characters are living beings like ourselves, breathing the same air that we breathe, enduring the same hard- ships that we endure, bearing the same sorrows that we bear and hoping the same great hopes that we hope. It is this very likeness to life, this absolute truthfulness to her model that is so great an attrac- tion in Mrs. Ward,that raises hek above almost all her ; contemporaries and that accounts for that measure of acceptability which is termed, and as I hold I incorrectly, her " popularity." ..11-,FttED WARD PERRIS.

THE TEMPERANCE QUEZA.

CHILDREN'S FESTIVAL AT WARRINGTON.

The time-honoured custom of crowning the AI ly Queen is not numbered amongst Warrington's recurring festivals, but its place is taken by a ceremony—the crowning of the temperance Queen whice is equally attractive and enjoyable. It is. some years since a union of the Band of Hope Societies in the town embarked upon so ambitious an undertaking, but from the first the success of the venture was assured, and there is now no question of its annual repetition. The inception of the scheme was greeted with remarkable enthusiasm in local temperance circles, and its continuance has led to a re-awakening of interest in the work of the Bands of Rope, and particularly amongst the members of the juvenile sections, and in the enlistment of the active sympathies of the children in the cause of temperance, its pro- moters achieve that which constitutes their primary object. Whilst the welfare of the children is the first consideration, a double success is to be recorded, as in striving after the one a spectacular display of an unusually attractive character is presented. This year the Temperance Queen was crowned on Wednes- day, the ceremony attracting a crowded audience to the Parr Hall, and all were splendidly entertained. _

Prompt to time, and to martial strain; from Master T. Barton's juvenile orchestra, the children in character costumes and headed by a diminutive minion of the law marched in processional order across the stage and round the body of the hall, prior to taking up their positions on the platform. There was an extensive variety of character represented ranging from a slave driver to a pompous ecclesiastical dignitary ; fairies, heroes and heroines of childrens' nursery rhymes and stories, Morris dancers, jolly Jack tars,—all were in- cluded and as they proceeded across the stage lime- light effects heightened the attractiveness of the spectacle. The appearance of the retiring queen (Miss Jessie Ellison) wearing her robes of State and carrying her insignia of office was the signal for an outburst of applause but it was rather unfortunate that the Queen elect (Miss Minnie Whitmore) who was of course the leading character of the evening wore no distinctive emblems and consequently did not receive the homage of her subjects. The applause was continued as the retiring sovereign ascending to the throne and took her seat underneath a beautiful florial arch. The ranging of the characters on either side was the signal for the revels to begin. With the greatest vigour the hymn "On this day of gladness" was sung to the tune of "Onward Christian Soldiers," and prayer having been offered the audience were abdured by the choir to "fight the drink." Next a scene of a village green was presented on which the children disported themselves, only to cease from their games to call upon the Queen of the faries (Miss E. Hamblett) to assist thorn in deciding upon whom to confer regal- distinctions. At her request, Strength (Miss G. Holbrook),Hope (Miss M. Butler), Faith (Miss E. Jones), Charity (Miss M. Muir) and Purity (Miss E. Shaw), recited their claims upon the throne only to be rejected in favour of Temperance. With true pomp Miss Whitmore was invested with the emblems of office and having seated herself on the throne,now illuminated with coloured electric lights, the others who had advanced their claims to the royal posi- tion paid homage to the newly elected Sovereign, who comported herself with befitting dignity. Now came the time for the children to display their accom- plishments and right well did they acquit themselves. The chorus' under the conductorship of Mr. Spruce contributed several items with signal success. Miss May Isherwood sang sweetly "Bells of gold" whilst the old time but ever popular Maypole dance was given by a number of girls. The effect of this and in fact of all the dances of the evening was greatly improved by the manipulation of the limelight.', The accomplish- ments of the girls in an umbrella dance were rivalled by the dexterity of the boys in a hornpipe. A folly dance and morris dance were introduced but probably the prettiest effect was secured by a cymbal dance in whica Miss Clara Holford formed a centre piece whilst performing the graceful evolutions of a Spanish tambourine dance. "See the Smiths" was well sung by boys,being accompanied by perfectly timed hammer strokes on an anvil. Some thirty little girls charmed the audience with the action song " Beautiful flowers" in which the solos were taken by Miss Dolly Cain and Miss Nellie Wheatley. "Cherry ripe" was sweetly and ,effectively sung by Miss Rachel Peacock, • whilSt Miss Jessie Woodland, another youthful soloist pleased her auditors with "The songs the children sing." A barbell drill by the girls was an attractive item and added variety to a lengthy programme. The efforts of the entertainers were rewarded by frequent outbursts of applause and with the chorus "The fire brigade" the curtain, miataphorioalty speaking, was rung down upon a performance as successful as any which had preceded it. A little adverse criticism is to be offered and this is directed not against the artistes but against the unfortunate lack of Continuity in the programme. Brief intervals were unavoidable but on several occasions the wait between the items was somewhat unduly prolonged, though otherwise every- thing passed off most successfully. Mention must be made of the efforts of two clowns who served to enliven the periods of waiting by their grotesque and, a bsurd actions which created no little amusement. The 'proceedings were Under the presidency of the MAYOR, who at the commencement delivered an effec- tive address. He spoke of the great pleasure which it gavp him to be present. The Band of Hope Union, he proceeded to point out, was re-constructed in 1899 with the object of educating and training children in the principles of temperance, and also for the supervising of the working of the Child Messeager Act. Twenty- one Societies were connected with the Union, which had a total membership of 4,000. (Applause.) His Worship pointed to the cosmopolitan nature of the Union and extended an invitation to all Band of Hope Societies to join. He was sure, he said, they would all highly appreciate the work of the Bands of -Hope. The educating of the children as to the evils of drink was the first step to take in the reform of the nation and he was pleased to know that a great deal more was now being done in that direction than was the case in former years. (Applause.) In his younger days he was an enthusiastic member of a Band of Hope, but never had he seen such a gathering as had assembled that night—a gathering which he was sure must afford great pleasure to the promoters to the parents of the children. (Applause). Mrs. SIMPSON, on behalf of the British Women's Temperance Association presented a beautiful bouquet to the retiring Queen and appealed to the parents to set the example to their children in the matter of temperance. A word of praise must be given to the officers of the union to whose untiring eflorts the evening's success was due. They were :—Committee, Messrs. T. Dale, G. Whitfield, W. Wilkes, G. THURSDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.. PRESENTATION OF PRIZES.

The entertainment was repeated on Thursday evening with great succePs, the hall being crowded. Mrs. James Dodds presided, and in the course of a short address said she felt it a great honour to be asked to preside over a meeting like that. The very sight of the chil- dren was in itself an inspiration, and as she looked at them the thought came to her that in a; few years these children would be the men and women of Warrington. She hoped they would be men and women who would speak with no uncertain sound on the drink question ; and who knew what distinction and honour the future held for some of them? There were in the House of Commons to-day forty members who were Band of Hope boys, and there was no doubt that temperance workers owed it a great deal to their influence that the Children's Bill, forbidding any child under fourteen to be served with drink, was passed into law. If these children were to be kept in the way they were now being trained in, there rested a very great responsibility on the parents to make straight the paths for their feet, lest these little ones be turned out of the way. Some time ago she read in an American paper an article entitled "A call to arms" which referred to one big city in the United States that was rising to protest against the drinking customs of the place. It went on to say what the fathers, the young men and the young women were doing, and added" And the mothers are pray,ing." What a tribute to the mothers! No doubt many mothers felt they could do nothing ; it seemed as if their lives were bordered by the four walls of a kitchen. They little thought what they could do and were doing for "more things are wrought by prayer than this world dreams of." (Applause.) Miss Jackson, on behalf of the "Y " branch of the I British Women's Temperance Association, presented the May Queen with a beautiful bouquet. _ _ _

later in the evening Mrs. Dodds distributed bock , prizes to the following children who had been most successful in selling tickets for the festival :—Ewart Grounds, George A. MILK LEFT BEIINDI

Farmer's Claim against flu Railway Company.

IMPORTANT POINT.

A singular ease was head at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before His Honour Deputy Judge Stracham, K.C., Edward Marsh, Bradley Hall, Grappenhall, claiming £5 Os. Bd. from the London and North-Western Railway Company for damages and loss through alleged wilful misconduct of the company's servant by neglecting to forward a consignment of milk at Thelwall station on December 16th last,such milk being consigned to Leigh and Bedford. Mr. Arthur Browne represented the plaintiff and Mr. H. Linden Riley (instructed by Mr. Fenna) appeared for the defendant company. Mr. Browne said the plaintiff was a farmer and was under contract to supply a quantity of milk to Mr. Stokes, of Redford Leigh, a large dairy retailer. He had also the usual contract with the London and North Western Railway Company for carrying milk at the owner's risk, with the exception of wilful misconduct on the part of the company's servants. The question, would be, he believed, whether the conduct of the guard of the tram at Thelwall station on the morning of Sunday, December 16th amounted to wilful misconduct within the meaning of cases on the subject or not. Mr. Riley: That is the whole point. Mr. Browne (continuing) said Mr. Marsh employed a man named Lockett to take the tankards from Bradley Hall to Thelwall station, a distance of about three miles. On the morning of December IGth, the train went from Thelwall station to Warrington at 8.19. Lockett arrived at the station before eight o'clock and went into the office of the stationmaster and gave the consignment note. He then went into the waiting room.because it was raining. The train was a very long one and the van in which the tankards had to be placed did not come up to the platform. Lockett stood with the milk tankards waiting for the train to be pulled up. The guard- that morning was not a regular guard but was a porter taken promiscuously at London-road station from which the train had started. The stationraastercollected the tickets from the passengers and then the guard, without any warning from the stationmaster, gave the signal to the engine driver to go. The train did not draw up to receive the tankards and, thereupon, both Lockett-and the stationmaster shouted out to the guard to stopi,but the latter took no,heed and never attempted to put on the brakes which he could easily have done to stop the train. Mr. Thomas Booth, of Booth Farm, Lymm, who happened to be in the train, would corroborate this statement. There was no other train on Sunday from Thelwall to Warrington until 2.30 p.m. and then there was no train to Bedford Leigh from War- rington until between three and four o'clock. Lockett therefore took the tankards back to the farm, and the milk was sent to Bedford Leigh by road—a distance of 171 miles. Owing to its late arrival, Mr. Stokes had to obtain milk elsewhere, and had claimed against Mr. Marsh. On Sunday, Mr. Marsh went to see the Thelwall stationmaster, who told him that it was not his fault that the milk was not taken. He did not give the signal for the train to go and it was entirely the fault of the guard. Headded that he shouted two or three times to the guard to stop but he took no notice. Mr. Marsh then told the station- master that he shouldfhold the company responsible for the loss, and the stationmaster said he would report the ' whole matter to his superiors. Alfred Lockett, farm labourer, gave evidence bearing out this statement and said he spoke to the guard on the platform before the train started. Thomas Booth, farmer, said that when the train started he heard someone shout" Stop." On looking through the window he noticed the tankards on the platform. Frederick Tillbrook, stationmaster at Thelwall, said he did not give the guard an intentional intimation to start the train. When the train was started, witness was under the impression that it was being pulled up to enable the tankards to be taken in. Witness called to the guard as he passed but he took no notice and the train continued on its way. He could not say whether the guard heard him or not. There were some empty tankards on the platform. In reply to Mr. Riley witness said it was possible that the guard might have mistaken a movement by his (witness's) hand after closing the carriage door, as being the signal to start. Witness did not regard him- self as being authorised to recall a train after it had left the platform. His Honour: Suppose the Chairman of your com- pany had driven up late. (Laughter.) Would not you whistle or to something to have the train stopped ? Witness: We would know the Chairman would be there. (Laughter.) Questioned by Mr. Browne, witness said there was no particular reason why the train should be got away unusually quickly. There was no hurry. His Honour: My experience of Sunday travelling is that no one is in a hurry, but the passengers. (Laughter.) I They fret and fume sometimes,

Eaward Marsh, plaintiff, was then called and said the cost of sending the milk by road was 255. The station- master told him that he and Locket shouted as hard as they could but the guard took no notice. Tilbrook added that he did not think it was possible for the guard not to hear. Hie Honour read the report which the stationmaster forwarded to the Superintendent of the line. It stated : "The farmer's man was standing with the cans and told me he informed the guard that the milk was for his train while the train was at a standstill and also after he had got in the van, but the guard ignored him. I also called his attention to the milk after I had returned from shutting the doors. The trainlad certainly started but no effort was made to bring it to a stand."

• Henry Stokes, Bedford Leigh, said he had to obtain milk elsewhere and when the supply arrived from Crappenhall he had practically finished serving his customers. He could not make any use of the milk,and

P—Elnazgz

it remained in a shed until it was unfit for use, when it was pat down a drain. Mr. Riley urged that the plaintiffs had not proved wilful misconduct on the part of the guard. It was only a matter of belief on the part of the stationmaster as to whether the guard heard or not and it was per- fectly consistent with his statement that it was only through-negligence or forgetfulness that the milk was left.

His Honour said that if evidence had been adduced to prove that the guard appreciated the fact that he was required to take on the milk and, notwithstanding that, started the train, it would have been wilful misconduct. He was not satisfied that it was sufficiently intimated to the guard that there was something to take on. He gave judgment for the defendant company. Mr. Browne asked his Honour not allow costs, as the case was being fought by associated railway companie. His Honour, however, allowed the costs, as he said it was always his rule to allow them unless the defendants had been guilty of reprehensive conduct.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church.

i The Sunday School Anniversary in connection with the Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church was held 'on Sunday. Sermons were preached morning and even- ing by the Rev. J. Gillender, of Chorley, the district • Sunday school secretary,whose addresses were delivered to large congregations. The morning address was based I on the text, "And ye fathers provoke not your children to wrath, but nurture them in the fear and admonition of the Lord." In the afternoon a children's service, was held, the chair being taken by Mr. Rowlands, the speakers being two ladies, Sister Louie, of Widnes, and Miss V. Taylor, the latter giving an object on flowers. Solos were rendered by Misses 14lay Isherwood,Rachael Peacock, and Jessie Woodland. The collections for the day amounted t to £8 12s. 6d. On Monday night a ' ' tea and tea-table conference were held, the conference rbeing introduced by the Rev.S. S. Ileashaw, the general Sunday school secretary. A public, meeting was after- werds held,the speakers being the Revs. S. S. Renshaw and J. Gillender. The Rev. C. L. Stowe presided. In the course of his address the Rev. J. Gillender gave some excellent advice to Sunday school teachers. The attributes of a successful teacher should be brightness, briefness and brotherliness. He laid particular stress upon the former of the three heads. If they had a true conception of the sacredness of their office and of the glory of the work in which they were engaged surely they could not help, but be bright. God have mercy upon those who gave-the young people the idea that to be sour visaged was to be Godly. That was a most erronous and harmful conception of Godliness. He appealed to them not to be merely teachers in the Sunday school but to enter into the daily lives of the children an join in their pursuits and pleasures. If they did that they would meet with wonderful success. r The Rev. S. S. HenshP....w said their first and chief object in the Sunday school should be to make the children into good Christians, to create in them a sound moral character and a sweet and beautiful spiritual life. He was sure that no loftier mission than that could be committed to the hands of men and women.

Bold-street: Wesleyan Church.

Special services were held in this Church on Sunday on the occasion of the re-opening after extensive decoration.

The pulpit in the morning was occupied by the Rev. E. J. Gawne, who addressed a large congregation from the words "On this rock I will byild my Church." Special music was rendered by the clArir, including the anthem "Jerusalem the Golden," an American organ being used in place of the large organ, which-is, being replaced by a more handsome instrument. There was an exceptionally large congregation in the evening, when the preacher was the Rev. J. Manning, who took for his text the words: "What mean ye by these stones ?" Mr. Manning showed what the churches stood for in human life- and what their message and meaning was to the world. Alluding to the decorations, he said he thought it would be generally admitted that the Church needed renovation. The scheme of treat- ment had been made a matter of considerable thought and care on the part of those responsible for it, and thej were all greatly pleased with the result, which reflected the highest credit on the architect and the decorators. The only incomplete part of the scheme was the want of the-new organ, which would, it was expected, be opened during the first week in July. The .whole scheme had involved an expenditure of £1,700, and they hoped to raise money not only by the services that day, but £l,OOO by the forthcoming bazaar. They hoped to start the new Methodist year in September quite free from debt. Mr. T. Brough's juvenile choir gave an admirable interpretation of a couple of anthems, "Consecration," and There's room for little children," and Mrs. Arthur Booth pleased the congregation by her rendering of "With verdue clad." The accompanists were supplied by an excellent orchestra, the whole of the musical arrangements being in, charge of Mr. Frank Hickman and Mr. T.Brough. Satisfactory collections were made. The appearance of the Church has been greatly improved and called forth favourable comment. Toe ceiling has been picked out in vellum tones, soft greens, and the beams a warm sienna tone. The rich cornice, also picked out in similar tones, brings out all the enrichments clear and defined. The richly carved deep frieze, (hitherto only distempered in one colour and unseen) has been this time painted a vellum colour, and Ithe background pinked out boldly in crimson, which with cornice marks has a very rich framing. The upper Iparts of the walls above the string line of windows and 'orchestral arch are minted a rich primrose tone, with a beautiful powdering as a diaper stencilled upon it, in soft greens and crimson. The orciestral arch has a companion arch formed on each side, and the two trusses separating the principal arch have trusses painted in hand, to represent the plaster one, These arches are enriched with inlaid orna- mentation in fiat, forming now a very pleasing whole, with pilasters supporting same in fiat, inlaid, touched up by hand. The walls through- oat are painted a plain soft tone of green as a base for the decoration above. The same treatment is carried out under the galleries to complete the scheme. The circular heads of windows have been glazed with Muranese glass amber tint, which gives a soft tint to the whole. The entrance vestibules have also come in for similar treatment. The windows, recesses through- out, and-the sashes have been painted a light terracotta and the angle moulds run in crimson, making a rich framino to the same. The seats have been washed, the cappings and pulpit re-polished, and the iron columns with their caps painted and richly picked out in gold and colour, and richly omolued, and the gallery front glazed down and re-varnished. The work has been carried out by Messrs. Hesketh and Son, Ltd., under the direction of Mr. Percy Silcock, A.R.1.8.A. (architect).

Archdeacon Spooner's Visitation. The Education Question.

Archdeacon Spooner held his annual visitation for the admission of churchwardens and sidesmen to office at the Warrington Parish Church on Thursday. In the course of his charge he said the problem, of education still remained unsolved. The provisions of Mr. Is/Manna's bill had spread Consternation far and wide. The enforce- ment of the clause exacting one fifteenth of the salaries of the teachers from the foundation managers in return for services rendered in giving definite religious in- struction would mean the imposition of a burden upon voluntary sources already heavily taxed. The conse- quence might be in many, if not in the majority of cases, the compulsory closing of schools altogether, or the handing of them over entirely to the education authority. Fairness all round with the conservation of parental rights was what they expected and demanded. No settlement of the problem before the country could be considered satisfactory and final which failed to respect the provisions of the trust deeds, and main- tenance of parental rights, full liberty of conscience, and the recognition of definite religious principles as the foundation of our national system of edu- cation. Whilst the principle of government by the majority was admitted, the rights of the minority, however small, could not be ignored. Referring to the report of the Ritual Commission, the Archdeacon said that to him it seemed chimerical to hope that any rubric could be framed as a substitute for the so-called Ornaments Rubric which would satisfy the demands of extremists in either direction. It might , be a wiser policy to leave the Prayer Book as it was, I but with the addition in certain places of explanatory notes, and allowing permissive powers of liberal inter- , pretation with some latitude in practice suited to the ' wishes of the particular congregations, and always with the sanction of the ordinary or diocesan bishop. The ignoring of his lawful authority, a distinct violation of ordination vows, when the demands or requests of that authority were in accordance with the decisions arrived at by ecclesiastical tribunals, as in the case of the Lincoln judgment, was unintelligible. Resignation they could understand • submission under protest they could respect ; but defiance was unconstitutional and unchnrchmanlike. Disestablishment of the church in Wales was again in the air and behind it loomed the spectre of possible disestablishment of the Church of England. Disunion within, the absence of proper discipline, a want of external cohesion and consolidation of forces would Con- tribute more than anything else towards rendering both an accomplished fact—which God forbid. The Bill permitting marriage with a deceased wife's

sister and involving other possible alliances not now recognized as legal, if it became law would place many churchmen in an invidious and difficult position, unless a common principle and line of action be adopted under the direction of the whole Bench of Bishops and , endorsed by the representatives of every school of thought. The Archdeacon concluded by referring to matters of particular interest to the Diocese and to the Workmen's Compensation Act. The incumbent would be looked to for compensation in cases of accident to the curate, scripture reader, Biblewoman, deaconness, or nurse (unless paid directly and independently by some society or charitable organisation) ; and the wardens and schoor managers would be responsible in the case of the vestry clerk, verger, sexton, organist, choir (if salaried), organ blower, beilringer, church cleaners,caretakers of schools or mission-rooms, and in some cases the responsibility would fall on them jointly.

HOIST FATALITY AT WARRINGTON. Curious Case. Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest at the Warrington Coroner's Court on Wednesday afternoon, touching the death of Terence Rdeers (2S), cabinet maker, Dixon- street, who was fatality injured by a hoist at, the works of Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons on Thursday of., last week. Mr. Augustus Lewis, H.M. Inspector of Factories, watched the proceedings on behalf of the Home Office; Mr. H. J. -Westbrook (Robert Davies and C 0.,) appeared for Messrs. Garnett ; and Mr. T. S. Steel represented the relatives of the deceased who reside at Dowdairs Hill, Dundalk, County Louth. Mr. Chapman (of Messrs. Chapman and Brooks, Manchester) was present in the interests of the insurance company concerned in the case. Toe police report, read by the Coroner, stated that at 1.30 on Thursday afternoon deceased was proceeding towards his work bench, having to pass through a room on the first floor. The room in question had an opening into the lift shaft, the lift working by mechanical power. At the time deceased was going through the room the lift was higher up the shaf and travelling downwards. The lift opening was secured by a. wooden gate shutting off the shaft from the room, and for some reason unknown deceased put his head over the gate and looked down. The lift descended and caught Rogers on the back of the head, crushing his face against the rounded ends of the upright wooden bars forming the gate. Isaac Williamson., the hoistman, who was inside the lift when the accident occurred, said he heard a shout and on stopping the lift found the deceased with his head crushed. The lift had only jest started from the floor on which deceased worked and as there was a guard gate 4ft. 6in.high,deceased must have put his head in the intervening space and been crushed by the top of the lift. Deceased must have been able to see the lift in motion. In answer to Mr. Steel witness said the lift was used for carrying work from one floor to another and assum-- ing deceased had done some work it would be witness's duty to convey it below. He did not, however, hear deceased speak to him, and did not see him put hiz, head in the opening. Mr. Steel said his suggestion was that deceased was looking into the opening to speak to Williamson about some work. The Coroner: Have you cautioned deceased about doing the same thing over and over again? Witness: I have cautioned him two or three times. I have caught him putting his head in the opening when the lift has been above and I told him he would get hurt. Questioned by Mr. Westbrook witness said that so far as he know deceased had no work to be sent down by the lift. Deceased had been warned by another employe. The Ooroner Why don't the firm make the gate Oft. high instead of 4ft. ? Then if a man got his head over that, there would be no question for the jury and myself. Mr. Westbrook: No doubt Messrs. Garnett will consider that. In answer to the Foreman of the jury, witness said no signal was given when the lift started. The Foreman (Mr. F. J. Whittle) asked the inspector of factories if there was any custom with regard to the working of hoists. Had no warning to be given? Mr. Lewis said there was no regular rule which was operative in every place, but on various floors in some factories where the hoist was mechanical there was a system of signalling. As a rule the gates were auto- matic opening and closing as the lift started from or arrived at a floor. In this case the gates were not automatic and there was no signalling system. The Coroner thought the-lesson to be learnt was that the gate should be 6ft. high so that. if a man wanted to play the fool he could not. He did not say the deceased did so.

Nurse Moore said that deceased vas admitted to the Infirmary suffering from a fractured skull and died at 6.15 on Monday morning. Davi d Richard Hodge, works manager, said deceased had nothing to send down by the hoist. Deceased told him that he put his head over the gate to look in. Mr. Westbrook said the gate had been passed by a Factory Inspector. Continuing, he expressed Messrs. Garnett's regret that the accident had occurred, and their deep sympathy with the relatives. This was the first fatality which had happened in their manufactory for 83 years, and they had taken every precaution to provide against accident and for the safety of their Employee's. Mr. Lewis said that in his opinion a gate of 4ft. Gin. high for the ordinary work of a hoist was a reasonable guard. The real obligaticm under the Factory Act was that every hoist-way should be securely fenced, having regard to tiso nature of the work to be done on the various floors. Esom the nature of the work to be done or:these premises a gate of 4ft. 6in. high would be ample protection between any man and the hoist. What one foolish man did one day another might tio the next, and and he was sure the firm would be willing to make the gate Gft. high. The Coroner said he did not think many employers could say they had not had a fatal accident in their works for 83 years. It showed that the firm were doing the best they could for the safety of their work- people and he had no doubt that, in face of Mr. Lewis's remarks, they would make the gate Gft. high. There was no suggestion that they wefe to blame and he agreed with the Inspector that the present gate seemed ample protection. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death" and added that they considered there had not been proper supervision of tho working of the hoist. The Coroner: Is the blame to be attached to an individual or to the Erm ? The Foreman : We do not consider the hoist man exercised sufficient caution. We do not blame the firm at all. The Coroner said Nlessrs. Garnett were a firm of very high repute, and be thought they took all the precau- tions that could reasonably be expected. He thought, however, that it was very hard lines on the hoist man that anything should be said against him unless it was conclusively proved. Mr. Westbrook: The man seems to have done his best under the circumstances. The jury adhered to their verdict, and the Coroner said he was bound to accept it,

WM; HE DIDN'T COME.

A FOOTBALL TROPHY.

On Saturday the St. John's football team (champions of the St. Helens and District Sundsty School League Second Division) played the rest cif the League and added another victory to their list. After the match the Rev. A. Ainley, curate of St. John's Parish Church, presented the winners with a bersntiful silver cup, the gift of their President, Rev. C. C. Bardsley.-I.llr. Ainle y, in making the presentation, gave the young footballers some very good advice, which ho hoped they would always follow, and add lustre to the League to which they belonged. Thoy afterwards paraded the town, with the cup, which bore the inscription :—" Presented by the Rev. C. C. Rardsley 111. A., to the St. Helens and Distlict Sunday School, League Football Association, Second Division, 1906-7 ."

NOTE.--The most eminent operating surgeon alive said : —"lf the use of inward instruments were persisted in, they would have to build a hospital to treat the CANCERS and TUMOURS created by them." EXTRACTS FROM A FEW LETTERS WILL SPEAK FOR THEMSELVES.

DON'T PUT IT OFF ANY LONGER. It is important that any wishing to see her should lose no time in doing so. Your neighbours on every side can testify to the benefit they have derived from her treatment.

"WORTH ITS WEIGHT IN GOLD."

The above was the remark passed by another 19,dy in regard to one of Mrs. Slater's Belts. A STOUT LADY RESIDING IN CHESTER and who had an appliance about a fortnight ago for difficulty of breathing in walking, owing to the weight of her body, said :—I walked further than I had done for eight years, I felt to well ; I cannot say too much about Tour appliances." ONE WHO HAS BEEN GOING TO THE LIVERPOOL HOSPITAL FOR FORTY YEARS, WROTE :

"Dear Mrs.Slater,--1 have only been wearing your Belt three weeks, but it has done me more good than any- thing else. I intend to do you all the good I can."

-ANOTHER FROM BIiOUGHTO SAID :-

"Doctors have all told me there was nothing but instruments would do me any good. A friend per- suaded me to see you, much against my will, as I had no faith in anybody, and a blessing it has been to me that I did so. I There was a recrudescence PARLIAMENT of the Ritualistic question AND THE on Tuesday. We have not CHURCH. heard much about it since the Royal Commission on Ecclesiastical Discipline issued its report, but at a meeting of the House of Laymen of the Province of Canterbury on Tuesday, a motion was brought forward welcoming the report, and agreeing with its conclusions, which are, among others, that the law of public worship in the Church is too narrow for the life of the day, and it should be reformed in the direction of what is described at "reasonable elasticity." The resolution further went on to say that the House of Laymen welcomed the recom- mendation to issue "letters of business " to the Convocations, trusting that they will tesult in an amendment of the Ornaments Rubric, and in securing that greater elasticity in divine service, which the comprehensiveness of the Church of England and the religious life of the present generation demand. It looks like a plea for more liberty all round, but that is not its real meaning, as Lord HALIFAX was quick to discern. Under existing conditions almost any change would be a restriction, for the majority of

clergymen practically do as they like, either as to doctrine or practice, sometimes encouraged by their Bishops and sometimes defying them. Any change of law or procedure would almost certainly be directed towards the restriction of Ritualism. The hostile speech made by L >rd HALIFAX was of the firery and satirical kind, but it was inter- esting and valuable, as being under the iisguise of an exposure of what would happen under certain conditions, an unintentional but accurate description of the actually ex-sting situation. If, he said, the recommendations of the Cora- missioners were carried out—and clearly that was what the resolution had in view—the House of Commons, comprising Jews, incidels, and heretics, schismatics of every shape and shade, and a mass of men simply irreligious, would be invited to undertake the work of liturgical revision, for any law capable of being enforced must be passed through Parliament already. Under the conditions proposed Lord HALIFAX Contended, Mr. R'JTHSCRILD would diseuss the legality of the crucifix, Mr. LLOYD GEORGE would uphold the cope as the right garment for the com- munion aervice, while Mr. MASTERMAN would no doubt plead for chasuble, amice, maniple, and stole. Mr. MORLEY might argue for the disuse of the Athanasian creed with Mr. HALDANE, who esteemed it the most philo- sophical attempt to express the inexpressible. The language of prayer would be revised by men who believed that they died like dogs. The creeds would be overhauled by men who denied the existence of God—with more to the same effect. It is highly unlikely that any of the gentlemen named would do anything of the kind described, but if they were disposed to do so, what is to prevent them? Parliament interferes as little as possible with the doctrine and practice of the Church, but it could interfere on the large scale if it chose to do so. We agree with Lord HALIFAX that this ought not to be. It is illogical and absurd. A Church certainly ought not to be under the con- trol of a Parliament which contains men of almost all religious faiths, which has had members who were Mahommedans and Parsees, and other members who rejected all religious beliefs whatever. But what would Lord HALIFAX have? These are the conditions under which the Established Church is able to profess that it is the Church of the nation. There is only one way in which the Church of England can escape Parliamentary control. Lord HALIFAX knows what that is, and we commend it to him and to the other members of the House of Laymen as the only means by which the life of the Church can be broadened out commensurately with the religious life of the nation. Those who are demand- ing freedom to worship as they please, can get as much freedom as they like through disestablish- ment.

The game of politics often PARR-A-PHRASEOLOGY. strikes us as being a queer one, and certainly one of the strangest features in it is the way each side accuses the other of misrepresentation, and even worse things. All the charges cannot be ti ue, and probably a fair proportion are untrae. Possibly they fall largely on deaf ears, and at any rate there is no judge and jury to try their merits and demerits. There were some fair samples of politi- cal " charges" at the Grappenhall Primrose League meeting last wo.-1, the most terrible being uttered by the ruling councillor, Mr. J. Charlton Parr. His speech was of a peculiarly rancorous type and indicative of the soreness of the Tory wounds caused by the last General Election. Mr. Parr accused the Radical party 4$ in -- old-fashioned phraseology." of evil. speaking, . . . .

lying and slandering. this is one of the con.: solations allowed to the bruised and battered side. We might not have commented upon Mr. Parr's thus airing his op;nion of R,dical utterances, if he had not tarred himself with his own brush. The spectacle was too good to be missed. Mrl Parr raked up an old slander about the Premier— one that no one with a grain of common-sense believes. It was the old " metheds of barbarism" bogey unblushingly mis-used. Alluding to the conduct of our soldiers in the late war, Mr. Parr deliberately stated that " Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman characterised their exploits as methods of barbarism." Did he ? Could Mr. Parr honestly say " yes "to the query? Sir Henry has more than once denied such a use of the phrase, and his attention having been called to Mr. Parr's charge he has replied that" he does not think it worth while to take any notice of speeches of this description." We quite agree. It is not, but it is just as well to nail false coin to the counter whenever you come across it, or it may keep in circulation to the detriment of all who handle it. The Government likewise came in for consider- able abuse from the brotherhood of the sweet and simple primrose. But the character of a Govern- ment is not so susceptible to harm as'that of an individual, and the present Liberal Government needs little defence from us, Its legislation has so angered the Tories that it is obviously on the right lines. Toryism represents monopoly, and monopoly is getting its toes trodden on pretty frequently nowadays. Mr. Parr did not stop short at even the Premier and the Government, however. He had a smack at the unemployed—and a cruel ' smack too. It ill-becomes a reputed millionaire to talk about "subsidizing the unemployed; in other words those who prefer idleness to work,and would rather be fed out of the pockets of others than earn a decent livelihood for them- selves I" We have read or heard of no uukinder or more callous reference to those whose misfortune it is to be unemployed than this feom the lips of Mr. Parr. There may be, and probably are, unemployed of the type under which he classifies the whole, but these are not the people whom the Government and the nation desires to ist. It is a deplorable fact that there are tiiousands of men who prefer work to idleness, who ore nevertheless unemployed. And they are likely to remain so until the people take a toll of unearned increments from unsympathetic mem bers of the capitalist class like Mr. Parr. Unem- pteyment represents a serious economic evil and the victims of it do not deserve such a cheap sneer as that we have quoted. It is possible to improve the conditions which are responsible for their mis- fortunes, and we are convinced that the Govern- ment of to-day means to so influence the march of events that that improvement will, ere long, be visible and lasting.

Th 3 visit to Warrington on LIBERAL AGENTS' Monday of the Lancashire VISIT. and Cheshire Liberal agents was an event of consider- able interest in our local political life. There can be no two opinions as to the value of the work which these watchmen of the party—as Mr. Paiker aptly described them—perform, and the fact that they hold periodical conferences for the discussion of political affairs and party organiza- tion, speaks well for their enthusiasm in the progressive cause. It was gratifying to see such a large muster for the meeting at Warrington, and the gathering was unique in that it witnessed !a presentation to Mr. William Lord, of Burnley, the retiring chairman, who is deservedly held in respect by his co-workers. He has laboured indefatigably for many years to further the interests of Liberalism, and in recognizin g his services in such a pleasing way the agents were rendering honour to whom honour is undoubtedly due. To local Liberals the most interesting part of the day's proceedings was the social function in the evening, when the visitors were entertained by Mr. A; H. Crosfield, M.P., the Borough Member had a most cordial welcome and delivered a particularly interesting atd thougetful address.
