NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The gallant fishermen of Hope Cove, who shinned down the cliffs and rescued 117 people from the Jcbba, have been awarded ;£1 each by the Board of Trade. They are now (says "London Opinion ") the most 1-Topless Coves on the coast.

The "definition of a lie" given in this column last week ought to have been labelled as a school-boy "howler," which it is said to be. At any rate it appears in an an article on the,-13 gems from the schoolroom by Dr. Macnamara, M.P. * * * He who owes nothing fears. nothing._—" Niumford."

Not by speeches and resolutions -of majorities are great questions decided, but by iron and blood.— " Bismarck."

At the root of all the steady pride of the bourgeoise of England is an irradicable awe and admiration the aristocracy.—" Dolf Wyllarde."

- So far as the Warrington Cricket Club is concerned their season commenced a fortnight ago but up to the present not a ball has been bowled. The cycling club have also suffered from the too evident humidity of the atmosphere but they number some bold spirits in their ranks and the official programme has been carried out if only by a very few. Fine weather is hoped for to-day when the unofficial run will be to Broughton.

* * * Delany Warringtonians were much puzzled as to the cause of the display of flags to be witnessed on Tuesday, Union Jacks and other emblems floating gaily on the breeze from the public buildings. After profound cogitation they succeeded in solving the mystery. It was St. George's day ! We believe our kinsmen from the Emerald Isle are much better versed in these matters, for St. Patrick's day would never be allowed to slip by without the " wearing of the green." We saw very few roses on Tuesday, but that might be the fault of the weather and the gardeners ! o *

A double disappointment befel Dover Socialists who had assembled in considerable numbers at the Town Hall on Monday night. Mr. J. N. Seddon, M.P., was announced to speak on " Socialism—a Dream or a Reality ? " but failed to arrive. After waiting for a while, the Chairman announced that Mr. Seddon had sent a telegram, asking them to meet 111% Richards, M.P., who had consented to take his place. A deputa- tion went to the station, but Mr. Richards did not arrive. In the hope that he would come later, the meeting sang Socialist hymns, and speeches were given by local men, but Mr. Richards did not put in an appearance. o

There was a meeting of the experts in political war- fare at Warrington on Mmday in the shape of the annual conference of Lancashire and Cheshire Liberal agents. They represent the general staff of the Liberal army, and if the warfare had been on all fours with military warfare, an attempt at capture by the Conservatives might have been anticipated. Forty- three of the smartest electioneerers in Lancashire and Cheshire would make good hostages. They were allowed to come and go without interference, however, and, we believe, enjoyed their visit to the "good old town "coupled as it was with the Borough Member's generous hospitality. 0 0

A case of much interest to farmers and milk-dealers was heard at the Warrington County Court on Thurs- day. Briefly, the facts were that a consignment of milk from a Grappenhall farmer to Bedford Leigh was left on the Thelwall station platform on Sunday, December 16th, instead of being taken by the morning train. As a result the milk was wasted, and the farmer claimed damages from the L. and N-W. Railway Company. From the evidence it certainly appears that the Company was responsible through its servants, but unfortunately for the plaintiff he had to prove " wilful misconduct " and this he was unable to do. It appears that the milk is carried at owner's risk unless "wilful misconduct" on the part of the Company's servants can be proved. Whether the guard of the train was guilty in this respect or merely negligent is an open question, and the verdict conse- quently went against the farmer. The latter is to be sympathised with, for the Railway Company appear to accept little responsibility for the carriage of so perishable an article as milk. * * *

A Salford parent has been complaining of the necessity for paying an additional sum to a profes- sional penman to have the children at the municipal elementary schools taught to write. He says : "Yesterday my daughter came, bringing a message, substantiated by an advertising document, in which it was declared that for three weeks daily half- hour writing lessons would be given after ordinary school hours at a certain charge." The Complaint is certainly a curious one. We always understood that handwriting was included in the " three R's" which are supposed to form the basis of elementary education. If the Salford teachers cannot, or do not, teach the scholars how to write, it is a sad reflection on present-day methods of training the young. There is Surely no ground for such a complaint in Warrington? Perhaps some member of the teaching profession will write us on the .question * * *

From a record of the Palatine Court of the Duchy of Lancester, it appears that in the year 1400 Thomas Graner, of Manchester, had constructed fir the Prior of Warrington a" horologium " which had not given satisfaction. It is disappointing to find (says the " Manchester Guardian ") that this early reference to a local industry should be contained in the customer's complaint against the maker of the article. What was the " horologium " ? The name shows that it was a timekeeper, but the word would apply equally to a water-clock or a siundial. Fortunately Bishop Reginald Pecock, whose "Repressor of over-much Blaming of the Clergy " was written about 1449, has a passage in which the precise meaning of " horo- logium" is exemplified. He is speaking of various ordinances as based on Scripture, and points out that many things which are lawful are not enjoined or even mentioned in the Bible. "In all Holy Scrip- ture," he says, "it is not expresssd by bidding, coun- selling, or witnessing, or by any exampl) of person • • • that men should make and use clocks for to know the hours of the day and night, for though in eldest days, and though in Scripture mention is made of orologiß showing the hours of the day by , shadow made by the sun in a circle, certes never save , in late days was any clock telling the hours of the day and night by peise and by stroke, and open it is that nowhere in Holy Scripture is express mention made of any such." So the horologium was probably a sun- dial. How the Manchester manufacturer and his Warrington customer composed their differences does not appear, but it is a fact in the industrial history of Manchester that at the end of the fourteenth century it was a place where sundials were made—even though they did not give satisfaction to the critical Prior of Warrington

The Warrington Branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children has during the quarter ended 31st March, 1907 inquired into 69 com- plaints of neglect, ill-treatment and other wrongs of childhood, of which all were found to be true, affect- ing 226 children and 103 offenders. Action was taken as follows :-62 cases were warned and 7 were prosecuted (convicted 7, discharged nil). The Society's Inspector made 411 visits of supervison.

Mr. Samuel Davies, the second oldest member of the Board of Guardians, having served 18 years, has been suffering from a very severe attack of influenza and bronchitis, and has been confined to the house for over three weeks. At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd., wire manufacturers, Church-street, were summoned for employing a young person named Edward Walmsley during prohibited hours. Mr. Augustus Lewis, inspector of factories, said on March 22nd one of his colleagues, Mr. Sumner, visited the defendants' works, and found Walmsley, who was 15 years of age, at work in the packing department. The Bench would remember that a short time ago a lad employed after hours at these works unfortunately met with his death, and he (Mr. Lewis) had had to prosecute the firm in respect to that offence. The Bench inflicted a fine of £2 and costs (12 108. 6d. in all). The Whitecross Company, Ltd. ,were summoned for similar offences in respect to three youths. Mr. Taylor (Messrs. Greenall and + 'o.) represented the defendants, who admitted the offence. Mr. Lewis said that on March 22nd Mr. Sumner, a stranger to the interior of defendants' works, paid a visit there. Presenting himself at the entrance, he met two men. One of them disappeared when he stated who he was, and the other accompanied him. Mr. Sumner asked if a certain place that was lit up was the weaving depFt- ment, and the man said, "Oh, they are moving machinery out of there. It is over here." The place they went to turned out to be the warehouse, and Mr. Sumner was misled. As they were going there, Mr. Sumner saw lads running about the yard, and two of them dropped over a wall.—Mr Taylor said the work [was done by sub-contractors and every effort to ketp youths out of the works was made. SAVAGE SENTENCED AT ST. HELENS.

A Preston man, named John Johnson, was sent to prison, on Tuesday, by the St. Helens magistrates for five months, under most extraordinary circumstances. Police-constable Rigby arrested Johnson the previous night at the fair ground at Parr. He was conducting a sale of cookery, and was drunk and using vile language. Several people complained of his conduct. A resident named Greenough remonstrated with Johnson, where- upon Johnson attacked him. Rigby then took him into custody. At the Town Hall Johnson was placed in the same cell as James M'Garry and Michwl Hennessey, who had been locked up for drunkenness. Shortly afterwards Johnson attacked M'Garry in a most ferocious manner. Police-constable Gray (clerk) was attracted to the cell, and found M'Garry's face in a terrible state, and the cell splashed all over with blood. Johnson was hammering away at his fellow-prisoners. M'Garry was called, but beyond exhibiting his injuries, could not give evidence, as he had no clear recollection of what happened. The Chief Constable said that at Altrincham in November last,Johnson got two months for half-killing a publican. When he was put inside the cell he there assaulted a prisoner most violently. The magistrates inflicted a sentence of one month for drunkenness, and two months for eash of the assaults— five months in all.

WHOLESALE SHOPLIFTING. Elizabeth Howarth, a respectable-looking married woman of 146, Peckershill-road, was charged with six cases Of theft. The Chief Constable said the woman , was arrested on Monday night, the 15th inst., and on the I detectives visiting her house at St. Helens Junction I they found a large quantity of goods which had been stolen. For some time the Cash Clothing Company and other shops in Bridge-street had been having a large number of articles stolen from the counters and door- ways of the shops. A special watch was accordingly set. On the 15th inst. the assistant saw the woman take the goods, and gave her into custody. She told Police- constable Griffiths that she was sorry she did it, and asked him to let her off as she had seven children. Five other cases were proved. Detective-inspector. Dunn spoke to tracing the other charges. Almost all the articles had been pawned at one shop in Sutton. When .he charged her, Mrs. Hovt .rth said, "I will tell the truth. I have only commenced to steal since baby was born. I was in great difficulties and had to do some- thing. I am guilty of stealing them." Her husband was an underlooker at Bold Colliery, but he had only had that position a month, during which time he had given her £2 a week.—Mrs, Howarth was sentenced to six months' imprisonment—one month on each charge.— The bench also ordered the pawnbroker to give up the articles without compensation.

WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

A CARETAKER'S POSITION.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee was held at the Town Hall on Monday when there Were present :—Councillor Fulton Smith (pre- siding). Miss Williams, Revs. Canon Morley Stevenson, J. Davies, N. Wilson and J. Yonge, Alderman Evans, Smethurst, Bennett. Councillors Steel, Toole, Ball, Dickinson, Nield, Ellison and Barton, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, H. Roberts, C. E. Parker. G. H. Kingdon, T. P. Bradshaw, C. S. Burgess, R. T. Fairclough, E. Plinston, G. Walker and J. Nloore Murray (director). A CARETAKER'S POSITION.

The Finance Committee recommended the terminating of the services of Mr. John Taylor, caretaker of the Parochial Schools. Councillor TOOLE moved that the minute be referred back. He could not help but think that the caretaker had been treated rather unfairly. If his information was correct no complaints had been made against the man during the eleven years he bad been caretaker, until five or six weeks ago. He understood that the school managers were in favour of giving Taylor another chance. Mr. T. P. BRADSHAW seconded. He suggested that the notice of dismissal should be suspended until the summer holidays to see how Taylor went on. Mr. FAIRCLOUGH said the man's work had never been satisfactory and the finance Committee had acted upon the suggestion of the Managers. It was true that the Managers were in favour of Taylor having another chance though he did not deserve it. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY strongly opposed the amendment. It was not the business of the committee to tolerate inefficiency in any department. Mr. ROBERTS asked how long the committee were going to tolerate hcompetence and neglect on the part of any of their officials. That was not the first time I they bad been asked to put a premium on incompetence. After further discussion a vote ,was taken and the amendment approved by ten votes to nine. The voting was :

CATHOLIC ENTERTAIN NTENT.s

An entertainment was given in St. Mary' Hall, on Saturday evening, by the children of St. Mary's Roman Catholic Schools, assisted by a minstrel troupe, from Windleshaw's Catholic Young Men's Society, whose jokes, stump speeches, songs and choruses kept the people in roars of laughter. The humorous interlude, "Coal and coke," by the Brothers Ripley, was excellent, and the stump speech by Professor Schlappembach was heartily enjoyed. The school chil- dren in their comic speciality " Mrs. Jarley's wax- works," deserve special praise, and the laughable sketch entitled "The burglary at Slappington Manor," brought the programme to a close. The Windleshaw Troupe gaye an excellent performance,and Miss Brown,the head- mistress of St. Mary's school, and her staff, deserve special praise for the training of the children, also Mrs. Dolan and Miss Swift, who acted as accompanists.

NEWTON URBAN COUNCIL.

The annual meeting was held at the Earlestown Town Hall on Monday afternoon, the chair being temporarily occupied by the Clerk (Mr. C. Cale). There were present :—Dr. Patterson, Messrs. T. Ormand, Coiling- wood, F. Harwood, A. Tunstall, T. Ritchie, M. Seton, J. Naylor and G. F. Clarke, A. Bowes (surveyor), and R. T. Surtees (water engineer). Mr. Collingwood said he had great pleasure in pro- posing that Mr. Ormand be elected chairman for another year. He had faithfully carried out the duties for three years, and they could not do better than re-elect him. (Applause). Mr. Clarke seconded, and said Mr. Ormand had rendered great service not only during the time he had filled the position of chairman during the past three years, but as a member of the old Board of Com- missioners. They had the greatest possible confidence in his ability to discharge the duites of the Council for what he had done in the past was a guarantee and surety for the future. (Hear, hear.) At the same time many membsrs of the Council no doubt aspired to the honour of occupying the chair. Every member should have an opportunity of attaining that honour. At the same time he did not wish to make a proposal which would interfere with the harmony and peacefulness which had characterised their meetings during the past year. He would admit they had differences of opinion at times, but they were differences which led to the best solution of many of the difficulties which came before them. (Applause). Mr. Cole put the resolution to the meeting, and it was carried unanimously. Mr. Ormand then took the chair, and said he thanked them heartily for again electing him as chairman, and for the kind words which had been said about him. He had much pleasure in acceding to the request of the Council. He had been greatly supported during his tenure of office, and had received the kindly sympathy and hearty co-operation of all the members. Whatever shortcomings he might have he knew that the kindly feeling which permeated throughout the Council would stimulate them to look over any failings of his *so that he might conduct the business as chairman of the Council in an impartial manner. (Hear, near.) He was pleased Mr. Clarke bad mentioned the quertion of a successor. If he (Mr. Clarke) had not referred to it was his intention to have done so. This was his fourth consecutive year as chairman, and he thought at the end of the present year he would have a claim upon their indulgence and ask them to release him from the duties. He thanked them individually and collectively for their sympathy and support during the year, and hoped they would support him in the future as they had done in the past. (Applause.) . . . .

The Chairman concluded 'by proposing the re-election of Mr. Thompson as vice-chairman. They could not possibly get a better man for the position. (Hear, hear). Mr. Clarke seconded, and the motion was carried, _

On the proposition o'f Mr. Clarke, seconded by Mr. Naylor, the committees were re-elected. It %%as decided that the ordinary monthly meetings of the Council be held as last year, namely, on the first Monday in each month at 5.15 p.m. The Chairman said he had received a petition from the residents of Wargrave relating to the state of the sewers. The petition was signed by 123 residents of Wargrave and Bradley-road, and it called attention to the unsatisfactory state of the sewers. Very offensive smells issued from them and they wished to draw the Council's attention to the matter. He (the Chairman) moved that it be referred to the Highways Committee. Mr. Clarke seconded, and the motion was carried. LORD NEWTON AND THE LORDS.

'Lord Newton presided over the annual meeting bf the Newton division Conservative Association on Saturd,ty. He said the Budget had been brought in clearly to re-assure the middle classes. The Government, warned not only by the speeches of Lord Rosebery but also, no doubt, by recent municipal elections, had realised that property was being alarmed, and the result was that we had a better Budget from Mr. Asquith than he, at all events, had anticipated. He had little fault to find with the Budget as a whole. It had always appeared to him that if you once established the principle of graduation there was no particular logic in stopping at the sum of one million and the only explanation that ever occurred to him for this stoppage at one million was that there must have been certain eminent and influential members of the Radical party of the time in the enjoyment of possessions worth considerably more than a million, who objected to being taxed more highly in consequence of that fact. He believed that the Budget would give fresh impetus to the fiscal reform movement. It showed clearly enough the utmost that could be got out of the Free Trade system under the most favourable circum- stances. It was sufficient to show the densest person in the country that in order to carry out the various schemes and promises of the present Government it would be absolutely necessary to discover fresh sourbes of taxation. Those sources of taxation were not going to be discovered under a system of Free Trade. Speaking of the reform of the House of Lords,Lord Newton said that the Government seemed in a state of some perplexity as to what they ought to do with this question. Attacking the House of Lords was not such an easy matter as people supposed,and the Government were beginning to experience some difficulty in making good their boast. He could hardly see what they were going to attack the House of Lords upon. Supposing an excuse was found, what guarantee was there that the country would side with the Commons. It was always assumed on Radical platforms that members of the House of Commons must be intensely popular because they were elected. His own opinion was that the affection for elected bodies in this country was very much over-rated. It never appeared to him that the House of Commons inspired the public with any extraordinary admiration or confidence. Nor did he feel sure that there was that interest taken in the House of Com- mons that was generally supposed to be taken. People, of course, bad different gauges of popularity. His gauge of popularity was the halfpenny Press. You would find far more in any widely circulated halfpenny paper about football or about racing than about the House of Commons. He thought we suffered from a plethora of elected bodies, and he believed that much of the popularity and much of the influence which the House of Lords enjoyed was due to the fuct that it was not an elected body whilst it was, in a certain sense, a representative body. From the fact that he had intro- duced a bill for its reform it would have been deduced that he did not consider the House of Lords a perfect I institution. Its defects were clear. It was too large a body. It contained a certain proportion of men who had no taste, or were, perhaps, not particularly well qualified for legislation. It was net sufficiently repre- sentative, and one party was always so predominant that, however just its decisions might be, and however fair and impartial,. yet appearances gave those decisions the impress of party character. But it was only from the *Unionist patty that real reform of the House of Lords could pro- ceed. The Liberal party did not care twopence about the constitution of the House of Lords. All they wanted was an assembly which would merely say " ditto "to the House of Commons. Whether it was composed of the most eminent people or of the biggest collection of fools that could possibly be got under one roof, it would be all the same to them, provided it was not allowed to exercise the right of veto. It had been said that he had introduced his bill at an inopportune time. He did not agree with that. The changes he proposed would, undoubtedly, have been more opportune had they been proposed years ago ; but,if they were required, that was no argu- ment for not proceeding with them now. What he hoped to arrive at was some sort of basis of settlement upon which moderate men on both sides would find themselves in agreement. What all sensible people wanted was a aecond Chamber which should be as efficient as possible. This could be obtained if the Unionist party showed itself determined to deal with the question.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in /malting it interesting and helpful to social workers. by contributing themselves. _

Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

The numerous letters of congratulation which the Editor of this column is receiving from week to week are sufficient evidence that the contributions are being read with interest and approbation and that the "Voice of Labour" is being heard throughout the town and district. We believe that our readers will agree that the various writers have endeavoured to briefly state their opinions in a straightforward and intelligent manner and that the privilege of ad3ptin!; a nom de plume has not been abused. That is as it should be. Probably, the column could have been made more personal. It has been suggested that, in future, the Editor m ght very easily refer to certain speeches of local gentlemen and criticise them from the labour point of view. In fact, his attention has this week been drawn to a speech delivered at the Primrose League by the ruling councillor, Mr. J. C. Parr, wherein the following suggestive sentences occur. "He could touch upon many other interesting points, for instance the reduction in the navy, the re-modelling, or as many think the practical destruction of the army. All for what? To get money for a visionary scheme of old age pensions and for subsidizing the unemployed in other words, those who prefer idleness to work,and would rather be fed out of the pockets of others than earn a docent livelihood for themselves. (Hear, hear.)" The inference to be drawn is that the "who prefer idleness to work, and would rather We Rd out of the pockets of others than earn a decent livelihood for themselves" are the poor, uneducated, unsophis- ticated bottom dogs. The people who live upon the, labour of others, however, are oft times termed "rich," and can well afford to do without a pension, but they are the only ones to-day who can claim—and take good care they get—a pension from the State. We would like to afld that such a statement, coming as it does from an educated gentleman, is to be deplored. We could say something, too, of the fact that the political pedestal of the local Conservatives is await- ing its appropriate statue, as was referred to at the Junior Conservative Meeting the other evening. It has been hinted that perhaps the local Conservatives are so ashamed of themselves for allowing Mr. A. H. Crosfield practically a walk-over that they will at the next Election allow the Labour Party to fight the straight fight with him, and will not intervene. Mr. Crosfield is not a Socialist, and it may teach him a few essential things to cross swords with a Socialist candidate.

JOHN WORKMAN.

" Walden " is a Socialist and be therefore has ideals. A Socialist without is almost as impossible as a Conservative or Liberal with ideals. It is passing strange but the public will read of Socialism whilst Conservatism and Liberalism contain the stuff that folks ere tired off. They lack finish; and neither is beautiful compared with the commingling of music and fancy and commonsense of Socialism. 'Listen ! A SPRING MORNING.

"Awake 0 North wind, and come thou South, blow upon my garden, that the spices thereof may flow out." Thus the song of Solomon, and truly we see signs that the glad invitation is receiving some response even in so captious a climate as ours. The fields and hedgerows wear the tender vivid green that fades all too soon as the season advances—the brave daffodil

That comes before the swallow dares And takes the wind of March with beauty is passing quickly, and the shy violet is hiding in the woodland glades. 'Tis Shakespea,re's birthday to-day as I write, and I cannot resist quoting his tender apostrophe to

- The violet in the youth of pritny nature, Sweet not lasting, fleeting, not permanent, The perfume and suppliance of a minute. The broad earth that William Morris loved so passion- ately revives herself in nemness of life, and one, looking across the long stretches of meadow and woodland, wonders why men should herd together in town and city slums. The earth reproaches us for the little use we make of her bounty and scouts us for the crass stupidity which allows her treasures to be filched by the few to the detriment of the many. I cannot be rapturous for five minutes, Mr. Editor, before some miserable reminder reaches me that even where land is plentiful, where there is plenty of space, the rottenness of our individualistic (so-called) system compels men and women to herd together in tenements worse than those provided for horses and dogs and pigs. _

A clergyman's wife writing from Devonshire says: "During the nineteen years we have been here 21 cottages which were then inhabited 4re now fallen down in ruins, and no new ones have been built. The people in a condemned cottage cannot move, because there is not a vacant hom.o. More cottages are sadly needed."

The cry comes from Hampshire :—" New cottages are rented before they are.finisbed." A labourer from Leicestershire writes :—"You have to stop in them until they are ready to fall down, and then go to the town or do as you can." A Parish Councillor from Huntingdonshire says :—" Cottages very small and squalid; railway carriages sometimes used as houses.' So the sweet country places that we generally associate with loveliness and peace are not free from the evils of overcrowding and isnanitation. But it is not more land we want ; but only the proper control and use of what there is both in town and country. Perhaps the proposals of the present Government may bring U 9 a few inches nearer the goal? I wonder. _

WALDEN.

HOW POLITICAL PRISONERS ARE USED IN RUSSIA.

The Dania Commissioners' report, which was read on Tuesday at St. Petersburg, and the truth of the major portion of which was admitted by the represen- tative of the Minister of the Interior, constitutes a horrible catalogue of tortures inflicted on political prisoners and suspects with the object of extracting confessions.

The report begins by stating that a special com- mittee was appointed at Riga for the purposes of tortures, and that the Government, with the know- ledge of the administrative authorities, the public prosecutor, and the colonel of the local gendarmes, empowered the committee to kill accused persons without trial. Some of the descriptions df tortnrts employed are unprintable. The report alleges that finger and toe nails and hair were torn out, and that prisoners were flayed with indiarubber sticks until the flesh was hang- ing in strips. Salt was then rubbed in. Oue young man who was shot after having been tortured had one leg almost cut off and his body covered with wounds. Another man was tied to a bench, a plank laid across his body, and men sea-sawed on it until the victims back broke. The police jumped on the body of a third lad until his chest bones were smashed in. The maimed wretches were generally shot near the Riga Central Prison.

" The Scout "has given us some very interestine , articles on Labour Problems but we think that, pro- bably, this week he puts the question, so far as it affects trades unionists, in the proverbial nutshell ! 1 Objectors to his scheme will please write on one side of the paper only and let their replies he sent in on or before Wednesday morning. The results of such a I scheme may or may not speak for themselves. The I objectors ought to.

ARE TRADE UNIONISTS SHORT-SIGHTED IN THEIR pourms ?

1 In giving an answer to the above question, if we are to be guided by local experience, then the answer must be in the affirmative. It is estimated that the/re are not less than 3,000 trade unionists in Warring- ton, which is an average of over 300 for each Ward in the town. If it were possible to unite them into one political organization,—and it would be possible if they were sensible —there is no reason why there should not be a fair proportion of representatives both on the Town Council and Board of Guardians. What fair-minded man will say that the workers have no right to be represented by men of their own class? At the present time there is not a single direct trade union representative on either public body. It has been urged, and no doubt will be again,that working- men cannot afford to give the necessary time to the duties of Councillor or Guardian That may be quite true, bnt is it honest to ask a workingman to devote his time to these duties in the interest of trade unionists, and to suffer financial loss in addition, by having, to attend meetings during the day, and to meet ''other expenses inseparable from the position of a public represent ktive ? The financial difficulty raised by opponents of labour representation is simply a" homey,' as I shall be able to prove. As I have stated above, it is .estimated that there are 3,000 trade Unionists in Warrington. If they were alive to their own interests, they would be willing to each sub- scribe a halfpenny per week to a local fund. Thi& would bring in a annual revenue of £325. The following illustration will indicate how the money could be used to advantage : ESTIMATED ANNUAL INCOMS.

s. d. By subscriptions from 3,000 members at id. per week ESTIMATED ANNUAL EXPENDITURE. . To rent of room for meetings 5 0 0 „ Secretary's salary lO 0 0 „ 20 Open-air meetings „ 2 Demonstrations in Parr Hall l7 0 0 „ 5 Election contests „ Loss of wages and incidental expenses (average ss. each per week) for 15 representatives 195 0 0 „ Sundry printing, etc. „ Literature for free distribution „ Postage, etc.

£325 0 0 The above illustrates what could be done by trade unionists' subscriptions alone, but I know from experience, that there are numbers of other men who would be willing to subscribe to a fund for labour representation on independent lines. Then again, it is quite probable that some of the representatives would not be required to undergo a contest when their tune came for seeking re-election. This would decrease the election expenses and provide a surplus, along with subscriptions from outsiders, which could be used either for public meetings, or other forms of propaganda. Will the trade unionists take action on the lines I have indicated? The employers of labour have recognised the value of combination, and formed what is called an Employers' Parliamentary Council, which is composed of both Liberals and Conservatives, with the declared object of protecting their interests as employers. We have also had numerous instances on our local Town Council, of employers of labour dropping their party politics and acting together to defeat the demands of Labour. Seeing thst the only property the working man possesses is his labour, it does appear to me that he ought to use every legitimate Means to ensure for himself the best possible return in exchange for that labour. Industrial combination is necessary in order to get the best results from trades unionism; political combination has become absolutely essential.

Let those who object to my sug,gestious state their views, and if they can prove other methods to be better then I am prepared to help as far possible to put their suggestions into practical operation. Nothing would give me greater pleasurA than to have my views criti- cised .by any of the leading trade union offi'.iials in Warrington. The more representative the official, the more delighted shall I be to" take him on." THE SCOvr.

TELEPHONE No. 4682.

The" Daily News" special correspondent telegraphs as follows—:

During the past week the mining houses have made private offers to the Government urging the renewal of the contracts, with the result that they have been defmitely informed that the Chinese must go as the contracts expire and any possible renewal will only be for a month or two. The mining houses have been warned by the Government that if they do not hasten to provide labour substitutes for the Chinese repatri- ated they will suffer, as the Government will not allow the mines to be closed.

Tbe Government now holds an impregnable position_ Never was it more certain than now that the Govern-- ment are determined to end Chinese labour, as whites, natives, and labour-saving machinery, such as the Gordon drill, are available as substitutes. A meeting took place in Johannesburg last night, at which 3,000 persons were present, to consider the unemployed problem

The- first resolution urged the Government to take more adequate steps to meet the unemployment caused by the five years' misrule on the Rand than by extend- ing the offer of the late administration of 2s. a day and food on road construction.

The second resolution favoured legislation com- pelling the owners of alienated mineral areas actively to exploit them, or relinquish them to the State ; and, furthermore, that for a period of two years any mine employing fewer whites or paying them a less aggre- gate wage than was paid for January, 1907, shall in respect of each month in which such reduction took place, as shown by the official monthly returns, be taxed at the rate of 5 per cent. on the mineral value of the mine.

Both resolutions were carried unanimously, and it was decided that the unemployed should march to Pretoria and present the resolutions to the Govern- ment. The speakers dwelt on the fact that they did not want relief work, but employment in the mines, and pointed out that Mr. Robinson was opening nine new mines. These resolutions indicate the trend of popular sentiment on the Rand.

MEMORIAL TO THE LATE COUNCILLOR

BROUGEI.

Fadgate Wesleyans on Sunday paid a tribute to the memory of the late Councillor Brongh when they unveiled a marble memorial tablet, suitably inscribed, which has been erected by the members of the Bible Class, as an expression of their esteem for him Its a leader, and an appreciation of the valuable services rendered to the class. The ceremony was prelided over by the Rev. W. Mellor, and was witnessed by a large congregation.—Mr. Bert Mason having read a portion of Scripture, and the choir having given a very effective rendering of the " Horne-land," the Rev. J. F. Matthews spoke in eulogistic terms of Mr. Brotigh, his special adaptation as a leader of young men, his bright, cheery disposition and intense sympathy leading them to go to him in their difficulties, trials and sorrows, and of the great loss all had sustained by his death.—Alderman Bennett emphasized this, and also spoke of him as an ideal citizen, taking great interest in municipal, political and social matters, maintaining before the world in all these things a Christian spirit and character.—Mr. W. E. Plinston (in the absence through illness of Mr. James Gandy) then unveiled the tablet on behalf of the Bible class, and formally presented it to the church. Mr. William Bennett accepted the same on behalf of the trustees, thanked the young men for their gift, and expressed, with deep emotion, the gratitude of all for so beautiful a memorial. The tablet, which is of white marble, and has been erected by Messrs. Williams and Clay, of Warrington, lears the following inscription :—" This tablet is placed here in loving memory of Walter Brough, of the Grange. Fearnhead, by the members of his Bible class. By bright example and constant effort be sought to lead others to that light of life, which illumined his own path. He received the call to higher service on 22nd November, 1906, aged 57 years." "It is enough that Christ knows all and I shall be with Him."

' A WOMAN VERGER.

An interesting character, has passed away at Alex- norongh in Lousia Harriett Dunk (64), who for twelve years has been verger at the Parish Church. She was appointed 25 years as a helper to the clerk and sexton, and on his death took over all his duties with the exception of gravedigging.

JOSH BILLINGS ON THE NERVOUS MAN. One of the most unkumfortabel kritters in this world iz the nervus man. He discounts all hiz griefs, and suffers more from trubbles that never happen than enny boddy else duz from trubbles that do "cum.

Hiz ears are like a-r-abi)lt's, always expectant of news of sum disaster, and biz nostrils are like those of the ass, snuffing misfortune out ov the east wind.

He steps az though he was walking on eggs, and lays down like a kat in frunt ov a rat hole, reddv for a spring. Tlieze poor phellows suffer without simpathy, and enjoy without satisfaeshun. The nervus man iz a long-lived bird; though hiz nerves are alwus strung, he lasts like an old phiddle.

Altho I lean't help but pitty the rlervus man, I am aware that he haz moments ov plezzure that are equal tew whole hours, they are so intensified.

Whatever he duz enjoy, he enjoys to the highest degree, passing the bounds ov reality; he revels in the illimitable' fields ov imaginashun and fancy. I think I would rather have more nerves than I could manage than not tew hay enny, and mope on thru life az sum men do, with nothing about me so excitable az mi relish for pork and beans.

One of the first requisites for a clerical career is an iron memory for names. Disastrous conse. quences followed a fottetfulness on the part of an aged clergyman, who was introduced to a "Mr. Eddy," and addressed him shortly after- wards as `Mr. Whirlpool." "How do you do, Mr. Net?" asked another mnemonic recorder of names-. "My name is not Net, sir." "I beg your pardon—Mr. Hook, I should have said." "'No, sir, nor Hook. I had the pleasure of meet- ing you at the Philharmonic last week, and my name is Bate!" "Knew it had something to do with fishing," muttered the mixed-up memoriser, as he walked disconsolately away.

THE CUP FINAL.

PREPARATIONS FOR TO-DAY'S GREAT MATCH.

Will Warrington Win ?

[By " Ovalist."]

It is said that some of the most enthusiastic foot- ballers in Warrington already see visions of illuminated tramcars and hear the blare of imaginary bands playing the march of the conquering heroes. Certain it is that there is a good deal of confidence irt Warrington's ability to beat Oldham to-day and so win the Northern Union Cup for the sscond time. Many of the waverers have come over to Warrington's side this week, for the severe defeat of Oldham in the League final last Saturday is not without significance. Signs of staleness were said to be plainly apparent, and if this is the case then Warrington should have a certain success for there is no staleness about the Wirepullers at present.

The local team was probably never in better trim to fight a great battle than to-day, for the men have had an easy programme of late and should enter the field at Bronghton in the very pink of condition. The steady training of the last montli has worked wonders, and the physical fitness of the men was obvious even in the semi-final a fortnight ago. Both forward and back there was greater pace and endurance, and the faster the game to-day and the more Warrington should excel. The forwards will need to exert every ounce to get the better of the Oldham pack. Posses- sion of the ball is everything, and that secured the Warrington backs will be able to make good use of their opportunities. Let there be little punting and plenty of passing. The open game is the only one which suits the Wirepullers and if they stick to it they should win with ease. \

Warrington and Oldham have already met three teams this season and the results are not very flatter- ing to Warrington. The two League games resulted in substantial victories to Oldham by 17-8 and 14-5, but the other match was a Lancashire Cup tie and that one Warrington won. They are certaintly more to be depended on in cup-tie than League football, whereas the prowess of the Oldham men has been manifested the contrary way. From what I can hear Olaham think they can easily beat Warrington again. If that is so they should get a rude awakening to-day.

===== 0002601_19070427_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
It is interesting to study Warrington's cup-tie record since the commencement of the Northern Union Competition. The facts and figures show them to have a record which probably only two teams—Salford and Batley —can approach. To-day is Warrington's fourth appearance in the final tie, while they have made five appearances in be semi-final and lost once —the first time. Since 1897 they have taken part in 38 cup contests, of which they have won 24, lost 9,and drawn 5. They have scored in the aggregate 383 points to their opponents' 112.

The records of Oldham and Warrington in this season's tournament are as follows:

OLDHAM. G. T. P. G. T. P. v. Runcorn v. Halifax 2 210 1 1 5 v. Wakefield 1 4 14 2 2 10 v. Salford

4 9:35 3 315 W ARRINGTON.

G. T. P. G. T. P. v. Batley 5 834 3 1 9 v. Hull v. Huddersfield v. Swinton

16 18 86 6 218

The Wirepullers' figures are the 'more flattering of the two sets, but Oldham have certainly had the hardest struggle, seeing that only the first match was played on their ground.

" Dan " Isherwood played wing in the semi-final at Huddersfield in 1897, and he has played in each of the finals since. He is the only member of the 1897 team still doing duty. There should be no hesitation about granting the popular centre a benefit next season. He has well earned it.

The players of to-day who have narticipated in Warrington's previous finals are Fish, Isherwood, and Baardman (l 901) ; Fish," Isherwood, Hockenhall, Boardman, Naylor and Thomas (1904) ; and Hallam, full back ; Fisn, Isherwood, Dickenson, Kenyon, three-quarters ; D. Davies and Brooks; half hacks ; and Boardman, Thomas, Shugars, Belton, Naylor, Harmer, Swift and Cook (in 1905).

Every one of Warrington's team for to-day has had cup-final experience with the exception of Tilley, Taylor, and Lees. The ex-Saffordian, George Heath, got his " baptism "as a finalist, when playing for the " Reds.'

THE OLDHAM TEAM.

An Oldham correspondent has sent me the following notes :

The final selection of the Oldham 13 will not be made until this (Saturday) morning. Seven forwards and eight backs were provisionally selected on Thurs- day evening. There is nothing unusual about the fact of an extra forward being bidden to be in readiness, hut the doubt is as to the constitution of the three- guar ter line.

"Birdie "Dixon is at present undergoing treatment for a damaged leg at Allison's Hydro, Ardxvick, and though his name appears in the selected 15 it is very unlikely that the committee will rely upon him after a fortnight without practice. It is more probable that Sam Irvin, the former South and reserve international, will appear at W.heater's Field. He is a steady man in defence though not such an attacking player as his reputation warranted one in expecting. Indeed, he has never fulfilled the promise with which he came North and has only played nine games this season with the Oldham first team,generally as deputy to Dicky Thomas in the latter's absence. He has, however, been doing excellent work with the reserves and is in good condition and a safe field and a good kick and a gallant defender.

The left wing is experimental, White being played at half back as a rule, though he figured at wing in the semi-final and scored one of the two tries by which Oldham reached the final stage. Arthur Lees will captain the side and he and his men are in the best of health and spirits and determined to make up for the loss of the League Cup by securing the other trophy. They are in much better form than they showed against Halifax. Rest and gentle • exercise have been the training this week and at the ,gene'ral practice on Thursday afternoon they looked a different team to that which went doll n so badly before Halifax.

The team will probably be: R. L: Thomas, full back; Tyson, "Billy" Dixon, Irvin and White, three- quarters; A Lees (capt.), and Yewlett ; Ferguson, Smith, Topham, Avery, \Towles and Wilkinson, forwards.

WORKS COMPETITION.

People who have closely followed the Warrington Works Competition have decided in their own minds that the championship is destined to be secure I by Rylands' representives. Throughout the clmpetition the team has played remarkably well and in all pro- bability popular opinion will not be falsified. Other teams have worked bard and deserved whatever success they have achieved but the Church-street men have shown a happy combination of a very creditable and effective character and in four matches have run up over 100 points. On Thursday thsy surprised Wilderspool Brewery in the semi-final and ran them off their feet. The game opened in a sensational fashion the winners crossing the line in the first minute, and a further try by Lloyd gave them a lead of• six points at half time. Within five minutes of the re-start passing amongst the backs saw the lead increased by ten points,Furness and Allen being the scorers, Philips kicking the goals. One of the Rylands' players was ordered off the field for rough play, and this somewhat equalized matters, but towards the close Gibbs added a try and a goal and time was called with Wilderspool defeated by 21 points to nil.

The nomination papers for Officers ani Committee of the Warrington Club and for special business will be on the n otico board in the Pavilion on the football field from 7.30 to 8.30 each evening from May Ist to Bth between the hours of 7.30 and 8.30...

Whilst Miss AtkinsOn, one of the guests, was getting into a carriage outside Christ Church she unfortu- nately slipped and broke a bone in her wrist. Miss Atkinson (who is sister to Mrs. Joseph Harrison) is very well known in the parish, and her misfortune has aroused mich sympathy. The bride and bridegroom have received a large number of handsome presents, includidg a brass gong and stool from the Girls' Class, a silver cheese and butter dish from the Mothers' Meeting, a barometer from the Sunday School teachers, a clock from the choir, a silver kettle from the day school teachers' and a roll top desk and purse of gold from the congregation.

THE LEAGUE

Saturday's match at Huddersfield of course settled the League championship, which has been deservedly won by Halifax. The latter have led the way all through the season, and to have been deprived of the honours by one match would have been hard lines. Still, Oldham have made such a qetermined effort of late that victory last Saturday would not too have given them the Cup, but the leadership only by a fractional percentage. Halifax made no mistake, however, and quite overwhelmed Oldham with a score of 18 points to 3 points. The League Table up-to-date is as4follows : Per-

Swimming.

The Warrington Swimming Club open the season on Tuesday evening next when there will be apolo practice for the juniors at 7.30 and the seniors at 7.45. All members desirous of taking part in the polo this year are requested to attend. The Committee are looking forward to a successful season and ate making strong efforts to improve the polo te tm. Some very attractive fixtures have been arranged in the Ist divisiop of the Liverpool and District League including Wigan, Everton, St. Helens, Southport and South Hill. The opening match is with Southport at home on Thursday June 6th.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Briggs—Fainlough.

A wedding, unique in the history of the parish, took place at Latchford Christ Church on Tuesday, when the vicar, the Rev. George Cecil Briggs, M.A.., was married to I iss Rachel Mary Theodora Fairclough, daughter of Mr. John Fairclough, Latchford Grange. This was the first occasion upon which the vicar of the parish has been married in the Wash-lane Church, and as Mr. Briggs is one of the most popular incumbents ever connected with the Church it was not surprising that the wedding attracted the greatest interest. Wash-lane was gaily decorated for the occasion flags and bunting floating gaily on the breeze whilst the parishioners assembled in large numbers. The seating capacity of the building being limited it was found necessary to issue tickets of admission and many who desired to witness the ceremony were disappointed. The east end of the Church had been prettily decorated with flowers, arum lilies, narcissi, almond blossom, dutsie, azaleas, and roses having been utilized with good effect by the Misses Fairclough and Miss Atkinson. Whilst the guests were assembling Mr. T. L. Fry, the organist, played a number of selections, :inluding "The Bride's March" from " Rebekah " Baray), " Communion " (Jules Grison) " Minuet " (Hy. Smart) and " Elevation" (Batiste). The bride was escorted to the chancel by her father and wore a very handsome gown and she was attended by three bridesmaids---Miss Marjorie d'Arcy Fairclough (sister), Miss Elsie M. R. Briggs (sister of the bride, groom), and Miss Molly Fairclough (cousin of the bride). The best man was Mr. H. P. C. Briggs, brother of the bridegroom. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. F. A. Screeton, M.A., Vicar of St. Thomas', Hyde, and formerly of Christ Church, Latchford, assisted by the Rev. H. H. Wright, Precentor of Chester Cothedral, and the Rev. F. F. Hicks, M.A., Vicar of St. Michael's, Chester. The choir gave effective renderings of Psalm 128, "Blessed are all they that fear the Lord," and the hymns " 0 perfect love," and "0 Father, all creating." After the register had been duly signed the happy Vicar and his wife departed to the tune of Mendelssobn's Wedding March. As they left the church they were greeted with cheers by the large crowd, and by a fusilade of fog signals on the London and North- Western Railway close by. The reception was held at Latchford Grange, in a large marquee on the lawn,and afterwards the Vicar and his wife departed for Brittany to spend the honeymoon. At night a dance was given in the marquee by Mr.. and Mrs. Fairclough.

Prince—Nelson.

The marriage was solemnized at Bold-street Wesleyan Church, on Tuesday, of Mr. Roland Prince, son of Mr. T. Prince, of Horsemarket-street, and Miss May Nelson,daughter of Mr. Robert Nelson of 35 Sankey street. The bride, who was given away by her father wore a white silk dress (trimmed with valuable lace), and veil and orange blossom. She carried a shower bouquet of lilies of tlie valley and roses. The brides- maids were Miss Edith Prince, of Warrington, (sister of the bridegroom), Miss Bertha H. Greenhalgh (Roch- dale), and Miss Mollie Nelson, of Rochdale (neice of the bride). The iev. T. J. Dickinson, of Rochdale, performed the ceremony, assisted by the Rev. J. Manning, of Warrington. Mr. T. Prince jun., acted as best man,and Ur. J. S.Nelson as groomsman. A. recep- tion was afterwards held in the Bold-street Wesleyan School, after which the happy couple left for Grange. The bride's travelling dress was a wine coloured costume. Numerous and costly presents have been received.

MY DEAR NIECES AND NEPHEWS, ft) I was quite surprised when I arrived at the office this morning to see such a pile of correspondence awaiting me. Letters, post- cards, parcels, etc., by the dozen, and all to be gone through carefully, so it was not long before I settled down in earnest, as I did not relish being up until one or two in the morn- ing, replying to correspondents and sending off prizes and bottles of Extract. Do you blame me? No lof course you don't. I am sure there is not a single niece or nephew belonging to the " Corner " who would like to' see Uncle Ned working himself •to death while all you youngsters were oh the way to the "Never, never land." The chief thing. I want to speak of this week is that I have not been able to publish any of this week's contributions, be- cause I had several letters, etc., which have been left over from other weeks, and to avoid disappointing any dear relatives, I am devoting the space to these. I really think I must ask for another column, as my correspondence is increasing weekly. I dare not allow myself any further space now, or I shall be unable to print all the left over matter that I have in hand. You must really excuse me this week, and trust to my making up for any lack of interest in my next. UNCLE NED.

NVELCOIE TO Connie Fuller" Ethel Stevens.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l enclose you a piece a oetry. It is called:— THE CHILDREN'S HOME. They played in their beautiful gardens, The children of high degree; Outside the gates, the beggars Passed on in their misery. But there was one of the children

Who could not join the play, And a little beggar maiden Watched for him day by day. Once he had. given her a flower,

And oh, how he smiled to see Her thin white hands through the railings, Stretch out so eagerly. She came again to the garden, She saw the children play, But the little white face had vanished, The little feet gone away. She crept away to her corner, Down by the murky stream, But the pale, pale face in the garden _ _

Shone through her restless dream. And that high-born child and the beggar Passed homeward side by side, For the ways of men are narrow, But the gates of Heaven are wide. MAY SYLVESTER.

Dear Uncle Ned,—You will think I have quite forgotten you, but I have not. I have been very busy. I am very sorry the last story I sent was too long, so I am sending you some funny bits. Hoping this will find you all well, DOROTHY MILLS.

THE POLICEMAN'S CROSS- EXAMINATION.

A short time ago a large factory, fitted with the most modern appliances, including the electric light, caught fire, and despite the most strenuous efforts of the fire brigade, was almost demolished.

The following morning a newly-appointed member of the force was dispatched to the spot, with a view- of ascertaining how the fire originated.

After closely interrogating the manager of the factory, he asked to see the man who was responsible , for the eleetric light. The manager stated that the electric switches were under his sole control.

P.C.: "Then you are the man that lights up the electric affair?"

Manager: "That is so." P.C. (bubbling over with excitement): "Now, be careful how you answer my next question, 'cos if it ain't satisfactory, it will be took as evidence against ye-: When you lighted the electric light last night, where did you throw the match ? "

"The landlord called for the rint to-day. Micky." "An' phat did ye say?" "Oi told 'im the rint was in your coat, and Oi was so sorry ye were out withit."

THE MORNING WALK. Rover and Floss are two happy dogs; They are fond of their mistress dear. And so long as. she goes with them by her side, ,She never has need to fear. "Up in the morning so early, She takes them both out for a run; They bark with delight, and they race o'er the grass, And they always have capital fun. Sometimes on a bright sunny morning She will give them a swim in the lake, And laugh at them both when they come to the shore, And give give themselves each a good shake. Then Rover will carry his mistress's whip, And Floss then must carry her glove; There .are nowhere two dogs quite so happy as they When they're out with the mistress they love.

AMY BENTLEY.

Youngster: "Father, I am not sitting on the lowest form now." ..

Parent: "That's right! Here's a shilling for you. But tell me how you came to pass on to the higher form,"

Youngster: "The lowest form is being painted."

Teacher: "And why are you so late, Tommy ? " Tommy: "If you please, teacher, it is mother's washing-day.; she lost the lid of the copper, so I have been sitting on the top to keep the steam in ! "

What game do the waves play at ?—Answer : Pitch and toss.

NOT LEAVING WELL ALONE.

An Irishman took a contract to dig a public well. When he had dug about twenty-five feet down, he came one morning, and found the side had fallen in. Pat looked cautiously round, and saw that no one was near; then he took off hie hat and coat, and hung them on the windlass, crawled into some bushes, and waited events. In a short time, the citizens discovered that the well had fallen in; and, seeing Pat's hat and coat on the windlass, they supposed he was at the bottom of the excavation. Only a few hours of brisk digging cleared the loose earth from the well. Just as the eager citizens had reached the bottom, and were wondering where the body was, Pat came walking out of the bushes, and good-naturedly thanked them for relieving him of a sorry job. Some of the tired digens were disgusted, but the joke was too good to allow of anything more than a hearty laugh.

OLPTI KINGDON.

===== 0002601_19070427_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FOOL IN THE CARRIAGE.

When you are travelling there is always a fool in the carriage. He is the man who opens the window every time the train slows up at a station, and plunges out up to his shoulders to see what is going on. If the thermometer is twenty degrees below zero he opens the window the minute he hears the whistle, and he keeps it open until the station is out of sight.

He always sits in the seat directly in front of you. and it only takes him three stations to burden you with a cold that will last you till spring, and then have a balance to carry over for the next winter's account.

He never has any liminess at a station; he never sees anybody he knows; he doesn't know a soul' on the line of the road; he never sees anything when he does look out; but all the same he never fails to look out. His own station is the last one he looks at, and when he gets out of the carriage he leaves the window open.

+

"There are many things in this world to which we must shut our eyes," said the paternal Gibson. "Yes. pa, and soap is one of them," chimed in the pride of the family. , - ,

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending a story, called, "The Travellers and the Bear." My brother Louis would like to become one of your Nephews.

- Two friends were travelling on the, same road together, when they met with a bear. The one, in great fear, without a thought of his companion, climbed up into a, tree and hid himself. The other, seeing- that he had no chance, single-handed, against the bear, had nothing left but to throw himself on the ground and feign to be dead, for he had heard that a bear will never touch a dead body. As he thus lay, the bear came up to his head, muzzling and snuffing at his nese and ears and heart; but the man immovabry held his breath, and the beast, supposing him to be dead, walked away. When the bear was fairly out. of sight, his companion came down out of the tree, and asked what it was the bear whis- pered to him, "for," said he, "I observed he put his mouth very close to your ear." "Why," replied the other, "it- was no great secret; he only bade me have a care hew I kept company with those who, when they get into difficulty, leave their friends in the lurch."

I remain, your loving Niece, HILDA HENCHCLIFFE.

• "Young man," said the merchant to the prospective offfce-boy, "are you fairly well educated?" "I be," replied the boy, proudly.

Monkeys ,are quick to learn politeness and relined manners, for nothing seems to please them so much as to copy the ways of those about them. It is easy to teach them to eat with knife and fork, to drink from a cup or glass, and to use a napkin; they like it, too, and soon relish our food, and show likes and dislikes as strong as the most notional "spoiled child" in England.

CRUSADE FOR LACID p737,1,qm v_vkA 2

A VALUATI3IIBiLL

Speeches by the Premier and Srr John Brunner.

! Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman ' was entertained to luncheon by the National Land and Housing Associa- tion in the Holborn Restaurant, London, on Saturday afternoon. The company numbered over 500 ladies and gentlemen, including about 100 members of Parliament. I.;ir John Brunner M.P., occupied the chair, and was supported by Earl Carrington (President of the Board of Agriculture), Earl Crewe, Captain Sinclair, M.P., Mr. Winston Churchill, the Lord Advocate, Sir %V. 'Foster, M.P., Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., Mr. A. Billson, M.P., Mr. Russell Rea, M.P., air. E. G. Hemmerde, M.P., the Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, Dr. Macnarnara, M.P., Mr. Runciman, M.P., Mr. H. Samuel, M.P., Major Seely, M.P. Mr. A. Ure, M.P., Sir W. Robson, MY., Mr. Rufus Isaacs, M.P., Mr. C. P. Trevelyan, M.P., Mr. G. N. Barnes, M.P. Mr. John Ward, M.P., Mr. A. H. Scott, M.P., Mr. A. Mond, M.P., Mr. %V. R. Rea, M.P., Mr. C. S. Henry, M.P., Mr. H. Fullerton, M.P., Mr.T. H. Jones, M.P., Sir A. Biges, Mr. J. Massie, M.P., Mr. J. F. Brunner, M.P., Mr. A. Lyulph Stanley, M.P., Mr. G. Nicholls, M.P., Mr. W. P. Byles, M.P., and Mr. F. Maddison, M.P. _

The Chairman, in proposing the toast of "Land and Housing Reform," said they had lately with pride in their hearts been watching the Prime Minister filling his place as the first servant of the Crown and the fore- most man in the Empire. (Cheers.) He had filled that place with a genial dignity and a dignified geniality all his own. One and all they acknowledged themselves, and claimed to be, supporters of his policy towards securing the unity of the Empire—(cheers)—knowing that for generations past, and believing that for genera- Joins to come, the British Empire would stand , for the > extension of human freedom—(cheers) The Liberal party had much to do, and it had been warned lately not to try to do too much. They were , reminded of a pregnant saying of John Bright's that they should not try to drive three omnibuses abreast through Temple Bar. That the Liberal party should not try to do too much was a matter that was past praying for. The Liberal party would always aim at the impos- sible. Long might it do so. (Hear, hear.) Why, if , they got all they wanted the millenium would come. I All they hoped for was that they, at any rate, should be in the middle bus, with, Sir Henry driving. (Laughter and cheers). In his opinion, the question of local rating had much to do with better housing. If housing I reform meant anything it meant cheaper houses, and in order to have cheaper houses they must have a I fair system of rating. The Prime Minister had already given his assent to the doctriae that to attempt with- out a reform of the system of local rating and assess- ment to deal with the housing question would be a mockery. (Cheers.) Take the case of the squire who built himself a lordly dwelling-house, which cost £lOO,OOO, and the question of rating came before the Assessment Committee, who would be tor the most part, the squire's tenants. The law said the house should be assessed at the rent which a willing tenant would pay from year to year. But when a house was costly it was not easy to get a tenant. Where was the tenant to be found for Eaton Hall or Chatsworth ? The cost of keeping up such a place was equal to an enor- mous rent, and it was most difficult to say what a willing tenant would pay for it from year to year. The Assessment Commitee treated such a house in the tenderest way. But suppose, instead, that ten houses were built costing £lO,OOO each, it would be found ' that the assessment of the ten would be larger than that of the one ; and if a hundred houses costing £l,OOO each were built it would be larger still, but if five hundred cottages at £2OO each were put up, then, as a rule, the assessment would be three times as large as that of the house costing £lOO,OOO. (Hear, hear.) They looked to the Government to put that matter right. (Cheers.) A Valuation Bill, such as they were expecting, would, he believed, secure justice in this respect. There should be independent valuers acting on uniform principles throughout the country, and they should ho instructed to provide that the assessment should have a close rela- tion to the cost. (Cheers). Then, and not till then, would the poor be fairly treated and houses become cheaper. There was 'another thing, go where they would, they would find that poor men lived on dear land and that rich men lived on cheap land. (" Shame.") As long as that was true "there was something rotten in the State of Denmark." (A voice : "In the state of England." Laughter and cheers). Had this nothing to do with the question of local rating and assessment ? He asserted very clearly that it had. In England and Wales, landowners, as such, paid no rates at all. It was the occupiers who paid the rates. That matter was different in that vastly better governed country North of the Tweed. In Scotland landowners did pay the rates, and this difference between the laws of the two countries wonld,he hoped, soon be swept away. Landowners would not suffer in pocket if the reformers obtained the benefit of the great reforms at which they aimed. They were not predatory; they desired to rob no man. He bad the authority of the Lord-Advocate for saying that the majority of the landowners in the North of Scotland were to-day grateful to the Liberal Government of 1880 for the passing of the Crofters Act. Now, if the Crofters Act, which created such terrible apprehensions twenty years ago, benefited the landlords in pocket, there was no reason why reforms carried in England should not have the same result. (Cheers.) Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman delivered a long speech. He said he hoped the Valuation Bill for Eng- land would pass into law during the present Session and that by that means they would prepare the way for a reform of the rating system of our urban areas which was so urgently and properly desired, and that they would at the same time very much simplify the process of the public acquisition of land by getting on record how much of its value was due to improvements and how much to the ; natural value of the land. (Mears.) That was an indispensable preliminary step. It was undesirable and unjust that there should be a diversion to private hands of a large portion of the results of improvements made by the community. (Cheers). He appealed to the towns for help in solving the rural question no less than their own present and immediate problems. Legislation could not create new forces; it could only hope to guide and direct forces already in existence. He hoped we should be able to do something to liberate and secure fair play for the great economic forces which had become clogged in these archaic systems of ours with results which bad been mischiev- ous to the prosperity of our native land. "I look behind the Imperial aspect," declared Sir Henry," to the character and spirit of the people; for in helping to free the land we shall be helping to raise the people. Happy shall we be if to us it is given to disperse the cloud of gloom which has settled over our villages, to enable the peasantry to lift up their heads and call their souls their own—(cheers)—by giving them some little foothold where they will no longer be tied men, but free. Happy shall we be if before our day is I over we see brightness and colour and pleasant homes for the people in our cities and the figures of well- nourished children playing in the gardens of our cities instead of the dismal and sunless streets, which are too often the only playground open to them. And to achieve this happiness you and I are resolved. I see it in the enthusilsm of the meeting. (Cheers). "We are resolved," Sir Henry concluded, "without baste, but vithout rest, to press forward on a crusade of land reform." (Loud and prolonged cheering).

THE NORTHERN LEAGUE.

Halifax win the Championship.

Halifax beat Oldham in easy fashion on Saturday at Huderefield, and thus won the League championship. Oldham had a strong wind behind them at the start, and the Halifax defence was tested to the utmost, but it proved sound against all Oldham's attacks. Halifax were the first to score, and the try was the result .4 a fine bit of combin9tion amongst ilk forwards. Avery's kick was charged down by Robinson, who picked up the hall and drew Thomas out of position,when he passed it to Bartle. Lees made a great effort to prevent Bartle from scoring, but was just too late. Little kicked an easy goal. Fergusson eventually .broke through the Halifax defence and scored, but just before half-time Bartle added another try for Halifax, who changed ever with eight points to three in their favour. In their last two n atches Halifax have been in front at the interval and then lost at tho finish owing to a splendid recovery on the part Of Oldham. On Saturday Halifax never ielaxed their efforts for one moment, and completely overplayed Oldham all through the second portion of the game. Little kicked a splendid goal from the centre of the field, and, after a grand bit of passing by the Halifax backs, Morley and Riley each crossed the Oldham line. Little kickel a fine goal from one of the tries, and so, by 18 points to three, Halifax won the match and the League championship. Over 13,000 people watched the game, and the gate receipts amounted to £772.

===== 0002601_19070427_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington's Last League Match.

Warrington played their last League match of the season at Wilderspool on Saturday in a downpour of rain. The gate was affected in consequence and not more than 2,000 people turnod out to watch the rout of Rochdale Hornets. Both sides were well repre- sented. Teams:

%Varrir.gton—Tilley, back Fish, Isherwood, Taylor and Jordan, three-quarter backs; Lees and Hockenball half beck Sbugars, Heath, Naylor, Boardman, Thomas, and Belton, forwards.

Rochdale Hornets—Brooks, back; Gibson, Walker, Ward, and A. J. Richardson, three-quarter backs; Scho- field and Riley, half backs; Dearden, Fitton, Stubbs, Richardson, Crabtree, and Turner, forwards. Referee, Mr. T. King, Salford ; touch judges, Messrs. J. Edwards (St. Helens) and T.Ratcliffe (Swinton). Warrington immediately became aggressive, and combination between Lees, llockenhall and Isherwood led up to the centre scoring. Jordan was the next to score after a fine run in from half way,Thomas landing a goal. Rochdale next had a turn, and Gibson was pulled up on the line by Fish. Boardman had an easy journey for the third try,which was again improved, and another brilliant passing bout ended in Isherwood crossing for his second try. Half-time score :—Warrington, 2 goals 4 tries (16 points) ; Rochdale nil. Turning round, Warrington immediately forced matters, and Thomas got in. Shugars completed a for- ward rush, which Fish improved upon, whilst near the close Heath raced in from half way, after a fine series of exchanges. Result : Warrington, 3 goals 7 tries (27 points) ; Rochdale Hornets, nil.

Observer Cup Final.

The attendance at the final for the "Observer " Cup on Saturday suffered by reason of the heavy rain which prevailed, but nevertheless a good gate assembled on the ground in the rear of the King and Queen Hotel. Both Lostock Gralam and Marston Old Boys brought a good following, and great enthusiasm was displayed as the teams took the fiald.

Marston having won the toss. they 'played with the wind and rain in their favour. Immediately they made progress, play hovered round the Lostock goal' for some time, but nothing resulted. Again holding the upper hand, the Old Boys' forward rank made a rush for the " Blues' " quarters, and here Foster sent in a shot which Stubbs' the Lostock custodian, saved easily. After several skirmishes near the centre flag Lostock were seen to advantage, and with fine combination, reached the Marston " 25," and a corner was forced. This did not yield anything-Substantial, but they still had the greater share of the play, and at last met their reward. The forward line were in close proximity to the Marston goal, when Hitchens, at the left inside, nipped in arid the ball was banged into the net. There was no further score up to the interval, when Lostock were more than holding their own. Half time Lostock Gralarn, one goal; Marston Old Boys, nil. The crowd had greatly Increased by this time and when the teams again lined up, there would be about 2,500 spectators present. It was not long before Lostock were again attacking. An offside goal was scored, the point being disallowed. From this time onwards the game became dreary, Lostock's superior play being occasionally interspersed with bright spasmodic flashes by the Old Boys. When the game was over Lostock led by a solitary goal to nil. Marston laid a protest at the conclusion of the match, but this was not upheld and, therefore, Lostock hold the cup for the next twelve months.

===== 0002601_19070427_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTATION OF THE CUP.

Mr. tdward Draper presented the cup in the absence of Councillor Fulton-Smith. He said that those present had shown their devotion to Association football that afternoon when they had stood in the pouring rain to witness a match which he thought had been an interest- ing and close one. The teams wh o had contested were old rivals, and' whenever they had met, the encounter had always been stubbornly fought. The Lostock captain thanked Mr. Draper, and said he was glad they had won the cup. The encounter had been fought in a friendly spirit, and he hoped their opponents would take their defeat as sportsmen. The Marston captain said that while he was not going to acknowledge they were the inferior team, they would take their defeat like sportsmen, hoping for better luck next time. (Applause.)
