CONFERENCE AT WARRINGTON.

Spaeth by Mr. k H. Crosfield, M.P

===MC

important and democratic work for the benefit of the nation at large. (Hear, hear.) If the speech itself was a masterpiece in matter, he could assure them that it was none the less a masterpiece in delivery. He knew it was customary in some quarters to talk. of the House of Commons as a `‘` babblershop " only fit for people with the "gift of the gab " and to estimate that gift rather low. (Laughter.) There was an old French maxim bien dire c'est bien pensee—" good speaking means clear thinking"—and if ever there was an example of that they had it in the present Chancellor of the Exchequer. Coming .very near home, he would allude to another member of the Government of whom one of the best judges in the House of Commons and one of the most distinguished aud capable men there, said Mr. Churchill was, last year, THE PHENOMENON OF THE _SESSION,

—great praise that,where they had a front Bench packed with ability, making it the more difficult for the individual in a special degree to distinguish himself as their friend Mr. Churchill had done. (Applause.) It was not only the work be did in his own office where he was never caught "napping," but what particularly impressed one was his wonderful grasp of all the main political questions of the day. His speech in Drury- lane Theatre on Saturday afternoon showed the com- mand of the whole question of land reform and housing which Mr. Churchill possessed—in fact when Winston was on the warpath he was a host in himself. (Laughter.) There were many more on the front Bench that one would like to refer to, but perhaps if he did some of their friends on the other side might say they were blowing their own trumpet a little too long. (Laughter.) Then ought they instead to turn to the other side of the House? He did not want to say any- thing unkind, but the contrast was rather woeful. (Laughter). He had heard remarks in Warrington in years gone by about the personnel of this or the other party. He did not think they need fear to challenge their opponents on the question of personnel— (laughter and applause)—but it would perhaps be better on that pleasant occasion if they passed lightly over the other side—that unfortunate WRECIfED AND DRIFTING PARTY

which reminded him of a derelict on the political ocean, tossed about by the stormy seas of controversy, without a rudder, almost be had said—without a pilot. Might he briefly refer to one or two of the constituencies represented at their meeting? They had had an interesting ceremony in the presentation of a testi- monial to Mr. William Lord. (Applause). He (Mr. Cruslield) would like most cordially and hewctily to congratulate Mr. Lord on this pleasing recognition of his work, which must be a source of legitimate and honest satisfaction and pride to him as long as he lived. For many years he had been connected with the politics of Burnley, which was not unknown to them in War- rington. He (Mr. Crosfleld) had Prevent and pleasant opportunities of discussing politics in his early days with the distinguished Member for Burnley, who once represented Warrington. The successor to Mr. Peter Rylands was Mr. John Slagg,whom most of them would know by his honourable reputation as one of the fore- most politicians in Lancashire. So also was his successor whom he (Mr. Crosfield) had had the pleasure of knowing for years and whom he not infrequently met, not in the gilded Chamber —(laughter)—bat in the lobby of the House of Commons which Lord Weardale—perhaps more familiar to most of them as Mr. Philip Stanhope—occasionally visited. If he (Mr. Crosfield) had met those gentlemen who were former representatives of that constituency, he might say that the present MEMBER FOR BURNLEY

was his most intimate friend of them all, and a more hearty, genial and capable and universally popular man in the HORSE) than Mr. Maddison they could not find (Applause.) He (the speaker) was heaping coals of fire on Mr. Maddison's head in saying that because the member for Burnley pitched into him now and then. (Laughter.) No doubt he thought a new member wanted licking into shape. (Laughter.) The other day apropos of some question or other which cropped up, Mr. Maddison said to him "Now Crosfleld,take care you don't get too much cankered by the corroding influence af capital." (Laughter.) The phrase had a most awful sound with it, but their was a fine flavour of alliteration quite worthy of an old Parliamentary hand. (Laughter.) The warning was so earnest that he had been wondering ever since whether their poor friend Maddison had been afflicted 'with that malady himself. (Laughter.) He had been speaking of Cabinet Ministers, but he would say in passing that they were far from being the only men on the Liberal side who were doing magnificent work in the House of Commons. Though he would not have the bad taste to indulge in invidious and. unnece3- sary comparisons, still amongst THE LIBERAL-LABOUR MEN

TRIBUTE TO CABINET MINISTERS AND LABOUR LEADERS.

Warrington was visited by Liberal agents from all parts of Lancashire and Cheshire on Monday, the occasion being the annual meeting of the Lincashire and Cheshire District of the Society of Certifi- cated and Associated Liberal Agents which was held at the Liberal Club. There were present :—Messrs. William Lord (Burnley), Fred Monks (Rossendale), A. Keith Durham (South- port), C. A. Walls (North Salford), J. Travis (North Lonsdale), Alfred Barker (Rochdale), A. F. Muir (Liverpool), James France (Ashton-under-Lyne), George Reed (Hyde), A.. Valentine (St. Helens), W. Beardsley (Heywood), C. Davidson (Bootle), Fred Harrison (Middleton), A. B. Spring (Bury), Fred Burn (Manchester), Charles Wood (West Salford), F. J. Downes, J. Howes (Warrington), George Massey (Stockport), J. Howes, jun. (Warrington), J. M. Home (Blackpool), J. W. Rylance (Leigh), Henry Gerrey (Bolton), R. Vickers and R. C. Vickers (Westhoughton) Joseph H. Hancox (Liverpool), James Long worth (Radcliffe-cum-Famworth),H..Hetherington (Altrincham), S. Boyden (Blackpool), J. • ayhurst (Damen), A. W. Adkins (Chorley), W. D. Woodall (Eddisbury), 3. Seller (Chester), R. H. Douse (High Peak), Sim Schofield (Prestwich), F. J. Arnold (Macclesfield), Joseph Smith (Knutsford), and William Handley (Northwich). Letters of apology for non- attendance were received from Sir R. A. Hudson, secretary of the National Liberal Federation, Mr. R. Barlow, chairman of the National Re.corm Union, and others. _

ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

Mr. A. Keith Durham (Southport) was elected chair- man, Mr. Fred Monks (Rossendale) hon. secretary, and Mr. A. P. Whitehead (Oldham) hon. treasurer. Messrs. Monks, F. Burn (Manchester), G. Reid (hlyde), and J. Longworth (Radcliffe-cum-Farnworth) were elected members of the Central Council, and Messrs. G. Massey (Stockport), William Lord (Burnley), and C. A. Walls (North Salford) members of the Executive Committee. PRESENTATION TO THE RETIRING CHAIRMAN. Mr. KEITH DURHAM presented to the retiring Chair- man (Mr. William Lord, of Burnley) an illuminated address worded as follows :—" On the occasion of your retirement from the chairmanship of the Lancashire and Cheshire branch of the Society of Liberal Agents, we desire to convey to you on behalf of the executive and members our keen appreciation of the services you have rendered to the society and our high esteem for you as a valued colleague in the cause of progress and reform in the county of Lancaster. For over thirty years you # have been actively identified with the work of the , Liberal Party, and the respect in which you are held by your brother agents is excellent testimony to your worth as an organiser, while the unique distinction of having been attached to the same constituency during the whole of your political life is a significant recognition from those with whom you labcur of the fidelity with which you have discharged the many onerous deities which have fallen to your lot. Our admiratioh of your abilities as an agent is only rivalled by our affectionate regard for the many personal qualities you possess and which have endeared you to all with whom you have come in contact. In parting with you as an officer of our society, we earnestly trust you may yet be granted many years of health and strength to serve the cause to which you have already given such conspicuous devotion." The address is signed by A. Keith Durham (chairman), Fred Monks (hon. secretary), and A. P., Whitehead (hon. treasurer). Mr. A. P. Whitehead gave a paper on Women's Suffrage, and an interesting discussion followed. TEA AT THE PATTEN ARMS HOTEL. SPEECH BY MR. CROSFIELD M.P.

In the evening the visitors were entertained at tea at the Patten Arms Hotel by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P. who presided over the gathering. Among local Liberals present were:—Alderman Tinnion, Councillors Evans. Wilkinson, and Clegg, the Rev. H. Dunnico, and Messrs. C. E. Parker, C. Jackson Holmes, E. J. Guest, W. Roberts, H. W. Guest, T. Pemberton, W. Furness, W. Bibby, S. Wright, J. T. Locker, J. Worrall, W. Roberts H. Gray, J. Critchley, J. Elwell, J. Morton and J. Willett. The health of the King was proposed by the Chair- man and duly honoured. MR. CROSVIELD'S SPEECH. hr. A. H. CROSFIELD M.P., veto was cordially re zeived, said he was very very proud to have the honour of welcoming to Warrington representatives of those who took so active and influential a part in politics throughout Lancashire and Cheshire. (Hear, hear.) He supposed he was right in putting the counties in that order. (Laughter.) He had no personal feelings on the point because he felt himself a sort of Jack for both sides—(laughter)—having been born in Cheshire and having 'followed the greater part of his i commercial career n Lancashire. They were met that day under pleasant political skies—(hear, hear)—and he thought perhaps the cheery countenances around that board showed that they were all alive to the healthy state of political affairs to-day—(hear, hear)—which they all appreciated the more when they looked back on the long, unsatisfactory, period of spend-thrift policy and very mistaken politics. (Hear, hear.) They would all, like himself .feel

for whom he has so great and deep a respect, he believed the work done by men like Mr. Maddison and Mr. Vivian—for whom in an especial degree last Saturday must have been a day of special triumph—would., in positive value to the people at large, bear comparison with the work done by some of the best men in the Cabinet itself. (Cheers.) Mr. Vivian was one of the busiest men in the House and it was almost harder to get an interview with him than with any crowned head in Europe (Laughter.) He would like to take that opportunity of acknowledging the assistance Mr. ilowes had given in the fight in Warrington. Perhaps even greater things had been done elsewhere by some of those who were present—for instance in the Knutsford division by his good friend Mr. Smith— (applause)—but at any rate they were proud of the ' result in Warrington and heartily thanked Mr. Howes for his work. (Applause.) A RED LETTER WEER.

Mr. A.KEITII DIIRRAM,the newly-elected chairman of the Association, moved a vote Of thanks to Mr. Crosfield for his generous hospitality, and for the honour he had done them by presiding. That was the second largest agents' organization in England, and he was happy to say that out of a total membership of 53, 43 were present that day. They attended those meetings at their own expense, and with only one object in view— to forward the interests of the Liberal party. He had listened with great pleasure to Mr. Crosfield's excellent speech, and he thought no part of it touched his heart more than the reference to Mr. Howes. (Applause.) Happily the time had gone by when agents were mere appendages of the party, drudges at the beck and call of every officer or of every gentleman who would like to be an officer. The agents were now called into the counsels of the party, and were consulted as to matters of policy and allowed to carry out the organization in their own way. That was a great step for- ward and he thought the agents had quite justified the confidence reposed in them. Last week was a red letter week in the history of the Liberal party. They had at the beginning the reorganization of the House itself which would enable the Government to dispose of the business in a businesslike fashion. They had had a magnifi- cent Budget. (Hear, hear.) The income tax was reduced and for the first time a differentiation was made in income, the money earned by the sweat of the brain and of the brow being more lightly taxed than mere dividend income. The death duties had been in- creased, and the national debt had been reduced ten millions, while provision had been made for reducing it still further during the current year. A foundation was made for that very excellent reform which he was sure the members of the Progressive Labour League were looking forward to—old age pensions. (Applause). What was not least, the Government had endeavoured to restore the national credit. Last week a Bill was brought in for the establishment of a Criminal Appeal Court and another useful measure was the Patent Bill which would ensure to the Englishman that the product of his brain would he used in England itself. Then they had a promise of land,reform in the shape of a Bill which would divide the value of buildings from that of the land and for the first time give the toilers an interest in the land. They had the principle introduced—and they believed Sir Henry would press it forward—that unearned increment should go to the people. Beyond all that, they had the extraordinary spectacle of the visit of the Colonial Premiers and the presence of General Botha was particularly a vindication of Liberal policy in granting the Transvaal the Constitution which was bitterly opposed by the Tory party. Mr. Durham concluded by congratulating the Warrington Liberal Association upon the fact that its organization was so efficient and that the attainment of a 1,500 majority had not been the signal for the dismissal of the agent whose services, it was evident, were very highly appreciated. (Applause). Mr. LORD seconded and the vote was heartily accorded.

Mr. CROSFIELD in reply said it was a great honour for a young member of Parliament like himself to be allowed to preside over a meeting of such experienced politicians. He hoped it would not be long before the Liberal agents paid Warrington another visit.(Applause) On the motion of Mr. Scnorrnn, seconded by Mr. Mosics, a vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. J. Howes, and to Mr. J. Howes, jun. for making such excellent arrangements for the conference. Mr. HOWES briefly responded. SPEECH BY MR. a E. PARKER.

Mr. C. E. PARKER said he would like to say on behalf of the Executive Committee of the Liberal Association how pleased they were that the Liberal agents had come to Warrington and spent a pleasant day here. They recognized the worth and work of the agents to the full. The afients were as watchmen upon the walls of Liberalism and had to be constantly on the look-out. He could assure them that they in Narrington did not accept a majority of 1,500 as the death sentence of Mr. Crosfleld. (Laughter and applause.) They had put him in Parliament and they meant to keep him there. (Applause.) They were very proud of the victory and of the Liberal Government. Mr. Chamberlain,—with whom they all sympathised in the illness which had laid him aside—once said that if the Liberal party were returned they would soon be hissed off the stage. They did not believe it then, and they did not believe it now. They had got a strong live Government at the head of affairs. He was speak- ing to a Conservative, who was a commercial man, the other day, and he told him (Mr. Parker) that although he was not with him in politics, there was no doubt that the Board of Trade was thoroughly alive now and information could be obtained from that department which they could not procure before. He (Mr. Parker) believed that the Government in the estimation of the party and of the country was as strong to-day as when it was returned. Let them long keep it there. (Applause.)

DEATH OF THE REV. S. WILKINSON.

A Former Vicar of St. Paul's, Warrington.

Many Warrington people will regret to hear of the death of the Rev. Samuel Wilkinson, M.A., formerly vicar of St. Paul's, Warrington, who passed away at Halton Vicarage on Wednesday. The deceased clergy- man, who was about seventy years of age, had been in failing health for a long time and at the recent vestry meeting had intimated his intention of resigning the living in October. He was held in affectionate regard by mony Warringtonians, his untiring work here for eighteen years bringing him into contact with various sections of the community. He was educated at St. Catherine's College, Cambridge, and obtained his B.A. degree in 1861. He became M.A. in 1867 and in addition he took an M.A. degree at Durham in 1876. He was ordained deacon in 1863 and priest in 1864 by the Bishop of Lichfield. He held a curacy at Perry-Barr, Birmingham from 1863 to 1865 and in the latter year he took up a similar appointment at Stockton Heath which he held until 1872 when he succeeded to the living of St. Paul's Warrington- He was exceedingly popular with his congregation here and it was largely through his efforts that St. Barnabas' Church and All Saints' Mission were opened. He displayed great activity in many branches of religious work and also acted as chaplain to the Warrington Union. Tn 1890 he left St. Paul's to take charge of the church at Timperley. Here also he interested himself in all local organizations and was an enthusiastic member of the Titnperley Cricket Chit). In 1905 he went to Halton, having exchanged livings with the Rev. G. D. Wray, his son-in-law, and he did an excellent work in the parish. As a mark of respect the flag at St. Paul's Church has been floating at half-mast. The funeral will take Place to-day (Saturday) and will be attended by the Vicar and other representatives of St. Paul's. There will be a service at Halton Church at ten o'clock and the interment will take place at Timperley at three o'clock.

The reports given of the various lodges were most encouraging, especially in the southern portion of the arPa, the Warrington lodges occupying a most con- spicuous position. During the usual order of business Bro. J. R. Griffiths, Warrington (Temperance Mission Lodge), was unanimously elected to the office of ' Convention Electoral Superintendent for the present term. The principal event of the day was the splendid report of the Grand Lodge Session held at London in Easter week, given by the able leader, Bro. ,T. Oliver. D.O.T. Judging by the number of members taking part in the dtscussion which ensued, the spirit of enthusiasm aroused should giye an impetus to more fervent and determined efforts throughout the area. A hearty, unanimous vote of thanks to Bro. Oliver brought a most pleasant and proatable session to a conclusion. The delegates from Warrington were Bros. Roberts, Griffiths, Savage, Crompton and Nelson

HOW WE STUDY ECONOMY.

Britain consumes 140,000,000 lbs. of currants yearly' Judged from a scientfic standpoint this enormous weight of currants is equal in nutritive value to 187,500 tons of lean beef. A considerable proportion of this vast quantity of currants is used in the making of bread. Economically minded housewives consider Currant Bread a most thrifty food. It keeps moist. much longer than ordinary bread and needs very little butter.

THE NEW THEATRE, WARRINGTON.

Our readers are probably aware that the contract for the erection of the Warrington Palace Theatre and Hippodrome at Friars' Gate has been let'to Mr. C. W. Davenport. It could not be placed in better hands and it is confidently anticipated that the building will be completed in August. From the rapidity with which the work is proceeding there is every reason to believe that the expectation will be realized.

We are informed that the foundation stone is to be laid by the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) on Wednes- day next, and there will in all probability be a large attendance of leading townspeople. Invitations have been issued for the ceremony at one o'clock and for luncheon at the Lion Hotel at 1.30. The Regimental band from Orford Barracks will be in attendance at the site and at the Lion Hotel and will discourse a choice selection of music. The trowel which is to be presented to the Mayor is a very fine specimen a Sheffield work of particularly handsome design. Mr. Ralph Johnson has kindly given consent for the trowel to be displayed :in the window of his premises in Bridge-street. Mr. Barnes informs as that he has received numer- ous inquiries for shares in the theatre. There is to be no public issue but he will be able to secure a certain number of 6 per cent. preference shares for the public of Warrington, should they desire to have any. Mr. Edwin Bradshaw has consented to be a director of the theatre in company with Mr. F. R. Roberts, and Alderman Bennett is to be the auditor.

It is gratifying to learn that the promoters desire that as much work as possible should be given to local firms. We have already published a description of the theatre and there is no doubt that it will be an ornament to the town and provide a first-class place of amusement. Seating accommodation will be pro- vided for over 2,000 people and thq fittings and decorations will be of the most elaborate description. The public will be pleased to learn that Mr. Barnes will resume his Vaudeville entertainments at the Parr Hall on Monday week, May 6th.

PRIMROSE LEAGUE PERSONALITIES.

MR. J C. PARR MISREPRESENTS THE PREMIER AND ATTACKS cHE UNEMPLOYED.

At a meeting of the Grappenhall Habitation of the Primrose Leagne held on Thursday last week, Mr. J. Charlton Parr (the ruling councillor) delivered the following remarkable speech (as reported in our neutral contemporary, the "Guardian.") Mr. Parr, who had a hearty reception, said that in once more addressing them as their ruling councillor on the eve of Primrose Day,he did so under somewhat adverse circumstances. He alluded on the last occasion to the change politically, which had come and the cloud which overshadowed them, and he could not say things were brighter yet. However, some of them might see a rift in the cloud,a bright spot whence the sun might re-appear, and that bright spot was the possible disruption of the present party in power, through its own extravagant and reckless attempts at legislation. (Hear, hear.) They talked a great deal about the filling up of the cup, and he could not help feeling that they were proceeding with that process much faster than they had any idea of. The crude and ill-considered measures that had been added to the Statute Book, together with the dangerous and mischievous Bill introduced and passed through the Commons, but happily revised and rejected by the Upper House, had caused such a feeling of irritation and uneasiness in the country and throughoutithe Empire, that signs were not wanting of revulsion and re-action, which might at the next general election cause a reversion of opinion in the electorate even greater than that they experienced on the last occa- sion. (Hear, near.) He was sure he was expressing their candid satisfaction, together with his own, at the visit of their Colonial Premiers for the conference in London and their concurrence in the hearty welcome they were receiving. (Hear, hear.) They would remember how the suggestion of the conference originated with the late Government, on the invita- tion of a great statesman,now unfortunately invalided abroad, but whom they were happy to notice by the papers, was progressing towards recovery, Mr. Chamberlain. (Applause.) They would also remember how the present Government, on acceding to office, threw cold water on the proposal, and did their best to neutralize any anticipation of good that was expected to come from it. But popular feeling was too strong for them, so they were tusking a virtue of necessity and extending an effusive welcome to our Colonial friends, with which they all joined most cordially. He could touch upon many other interesting points, for instance the reduction in the navy, the re-modelling, or as many thought the practical destruction of the army. All for what ? To get money for a visionary scheme of old age pensions, and for subsidizing the unemployed ; in other words, those who prefer idleness to work, and would rather be fed out of the pockets of others than earn a decent livelihood for themselves. (Hear, hear.) With regard to the army they all knew their present Premier's feeling towards it. -When their soldiers were fighting in South Africa,winning back for our Crown a land which a former Liberal administra- tion had gone a long way towards losing, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman characterised their exploits as methods of barbarism. ("Shame.") We really required a new vocabulary to explain the meaning of the speeches of our present rulers; for instance, the Attorney-General, Sir Wilfrid Lawson, advocated in a public speech a combination of the Crown and people against the House of Lords. This, in the usual acceptation of the terms would mean they would interpret revolution and treason to the Empire. He would, no doubt, explain it as loyalty and constitu- tionalism, but he did say that he thought the reporters had vanished. Then again at the last election the heads of the Radical party did not hesitate to mis- represent, to detract, and to falsify with regard to Chinese labour and other subjects. This) in old- fashioned phraseology, %ould have been called evil speaking, lying and slandering, but in the new vocabu- lary of the Liberal party it was called terminological inexactitude. (Laughter and applause).

Letter From Mr. J. Howes.

Mr. Parr and the League Criticised.

Sir,—The Primrose League is a wonderful institution. You can get more decorations and titles from it for about 3s. 9d. than you can get from any other organiza- tion in the country. There is always plenty of amuse- ment at its meetings. Its speakers are allowed to give the fullest scope to their imaginations. They are not necessarily trammelled by facts. They make their own history. If there is more than one speaker at a meeting their speakers need not harmonise, and with the assist- ance of the ventriloquist, comic singer, and piano soloist, they can always be relied upon to give a mis- cellaneous and mirth provoking entertainment. The motto of this institution should be" A little folly now and then is relished by the wisest men." Therd has recently been a meeting of the GRAPPEN- HALL HABITATION of the league, presided over by Mr. J. C. Parr, the ruling councillor. Mr. H. .1. Westbrook said that the league followed out the Biblical instruction, to train up children in the way they should go. Mr. Cary said they must attract the younger people because it was to them they must 'look for the future development of the party. Of course in catering for children they are dealing with that section of • the community possessing limited knowledge and little political experience, hence accuracy of speech is not an absolute necessity. Mr. Parr spoke of the cloud which overshadowed them as a party and said "He could not say things were brighter yet," but Mr. Cary said " Looking back since the days of the last General Election, they had at the present time abundant cause for congratulation." Mr. Parr, of course, strongly denounced the present Liberal Government. They were accused of throwing cold water on the proposal for the visit of the Colonial Premiers to this country, of reducing the Navy„ destroying the Army, 8.1 c., "and all for what ?" said Mr. Parr, "To get money for a visionary scheme of old-age pensions, and subsidising the unemployed, in other words those who preferred idleness to work, and would rather be fed out of the pockets of others than earn a livelihood for themselves."

Mr. Cary said they must "welcome heartily the workingclasses and let them be assured their presence W 9.3 desired," but I would suggest to these Primrose Leaguers that if they should succeed in getting any of the workingclasses to their aristocratic gatherings, it would be as well to suppress the utterances of Mr. Parr, for the toilers will not feel very happy when listening to the condemnation of the only Government that has made a practical attempt to provide them with a little money, during the period of old age, and thus perhaps, prevent them from having to go to the Work- house. Nor are the workingclasses likely to applaud very vigorously the man who tolls them that they' prefer idleness to work and would rather be fed out of the pockets of others, than earn a livelihood for them- selves." This kind of gospel had better be kept for the children they are training,and the younger members they are endeavouring to get hold of, who at present know little of the battle of life, net having had,perhaps, to rub against the sharp corners of adversity. To talk to grown up people in this fashion, would not be likely to strengthen the membership of the Primrose League. The remainder of the speeches delivered on this occasion consisted of the usual Tory fireworks and fake, and slanderous statements.

When their s)ldiers were fighting in south Africa. winning back for our Crown a land which a former Liberal administration had gone a long way towards losing, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman characterized their exploits as methods of barbarism." " Winnine. back for our Crown." If he had said winning back for the ALIEN JEW :%fIL LIONAIRE.3, or winning back for the CHINANIAN, he would have been nearer the mark. Of course Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman, never accused our soldiers of barbarism. This is a stale slantier; often refuted, the repetition of which only shows thle miserable condition to which the Tory Party is reduced, when they have to resort to such tactics for the purpose of maligning a political opponent. What about the Army? What condition was that oraanisation in during the existence of the Late Government? Lamentable were the revelations that came to us during the Boer War, and can the nation have forgotten that three successive War Ministers, under the late Tory Administration, attempted to grapple with the question of Army Reform and failed, and now that we have a statesman endeavouring to pro- vide us with a well organised and equipped fighting force, these Primrose Leaguers show their patriotism by doing all they can to make his task as difficult as possible. Mr. Cary, speaking on the Education Question, said : The final step to which the Radicals were driving as an alternative to definite religious teaching would lead to secularism, and from secularism it was a very easy step to Socialism, while Socialism was but a step removed from Atheism. Any departure ! from those sacred obligations of definite religions teaching that had made this country great, to which this country owed its prosperity and happi- ness, was, step by step, going to lead to the same fate that befell all those nations that died in the

LOCAL COMPANIES' AFFAIRS. Apolications Re Peerless Patents and Alfred Stanway, Ltd.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Deputy Judge Strachan, K.C., Mr. F. Cuthbert Smith, barrister (instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel) appeared on behalf of the liquidators of the Paerless Patents Co., Ltd., (Messrs. Randall and E. Bradshaw) to make an application, supported by the Official Receiver, for the sanction of the Court to a scheme dealing not only with the creditors and members of the company but the creditors of Samuel Booth. Mr. Cuthbert Smith said that up to Decem- ber 1905 Booth carried on business as a builder's merchant in the name of the Peerless Patents Co. In March 1906 he agreed to sell the business to a company to be incorporated under the name of the Peerless Patents Co., Ltd., which was now being wound up. The company was a very short-lived one. It was really a one man company to all intents and purposes and in May 1906 it was found impossible to continue the business owing to its liabilities. At a meeting of creditors it was ascertained that the creditors of Samuel Booth and of the company were more or less mixed up, and it was very difficult to divide them. At a meeting of the company it was decided that the concern be voluntarily wound up. On May 26th the bankrupt executed a deed of assignment for the benefit of his creditors but on the sthJuly a petition of bankruptcy was filed and the Official Receiver took charge of Booth's estate. The creditors of the company and of , the bankrupt were largely the same and he asked the !Judge to sanction a compromise, which had received the approval of the Board of Trade whereby, as soon as the costs of the liquidator:3 and the official receiver had been paid the creditors of Samuel Booth and of the company would be paid pro rata out of the estate. His Honour sanctioned the compromise. The next case was in respect of a summons taken out by the Official Liquidator (Mr. Grant Gibson) in the matter of Alfred Stanway, Ltd., for the direction of , the Court as to how he should dispose of the assets in his possession. It appeared from the official liquidator's remarks that the company was wound up compulsorily under an order of the Court made on February 22n1, 1906, on the petition of Messrs. Cohen, ' Weenan and Co., who a day or two prior to presenting • the petition had levied an execution on the goods of the company. The Sheriff, however, withdrew on a claim being made on all the assets by the Receiver appointed by the Palatine Court of Lancaster for the debenture holders. In the course of the winding up, facts came to the knowledge of the official liquidator which led him to question the validity of the deben- tures and ultimately an odor was made by the • Palatine Court delating that the debentures formed no charge on the assets. In consequence of that order the official liquidator received from the Receiver upwards of £5OO and he now applied to the Court for directions as to how the claim of Messrs. Cohen • Weenan and Co., for £278 should be dealt with. He ' suggested three alternative courses :—(1) Allow the firm to prove ratably with the other creditors ; (2) to pay Messrs. Cohen £l4 ss. lid, and to allow them to prove the balance; (3), to pay them their claim of £278 in full. Mr. Gibson pointed out that no claim had been made against him but he considered it his duty to bring the matter before the Judge, as he was anxious to Ido what was right and equitable. He contended that the second alternative was the proper one. Mr. E. A. White appeared on behalf of Messrs. Cohen, Weenan and Co., and claimed that they should be paid 1

===== 0002601_19070427_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
-__

in full. The law was presumed to favour the diligent creditor who did everything he could to get his debts paid. His clients had undoubtedly adopted that course as the debt was only incurred in September and Decem- ber, 1905, and as it should have been paid at once a writ was issued on January 9th. His Honour said the official liquidator had acted in the high-toned manner which the officers of the Court were always expected to adopt—the very highest prin- ciples of equity. , When they came to apply that prin- ciple of doing what was fair, he thought it demanded that the man who by his own legitimate efforts possessed himself of property in accordance with the law should not be deprived of his right to it simply because he voluntarily abandoned possession by reason ef repre- sentations made by another officer of the Court which he thought he was bound to act upon. His Honour made an order for Messrs. Cohen, Weenan and Co., to be paid their debt in full.

CEMENT AND ITS APPLICATIONS

A New Industry for 'Warrington.

Under the above title a most interesting lecture was at the invitation of the Warrington Technical Building Students' Association, delivered last Saturday evening at the Technical Institute by Dr. Prentice (of Messrs. Crosfleld's.) _

Mr. E. S. A. ROBSON, M.Sc.,Principal of the Institute presided over a good attendance and represontativeS were also present from the Master Builder's Association. Dr. Prentice at the outset briefly surveyed the history of Portland cement, reviewing the successive steps which led up to its discovery by Aspdin in 1824. He showed that all the obstacles, which had retarded its application to arts and industries had been primarily. due to defective knowledge of the chemical principles involved in its production. In proportion, however, as its manufacture came more and more to be recognised as a complicated problem of chemistry and engineering it had profited by the immense developement in these sciences until latterly by the introduction of complete scientific con- trol and by improved machanical methods, it had finally emerged from the empirical phase into its present position as one of the largest chemical industries of the day. By the aid of lantern slides,he illustrated the gradual evolution of the machinery employed in the cement industry, culuminating in the most modern type, such ' as was being laid down in Messrs. Crosfield's new cement ' works, now approaching completion. All these changes Ihe noted, emphasized the medern tendency towards labour-saving appliances and automatic devices, which. not only reduced the cost of production to a minimum, but by eliminating, as far as possible the personal element, secured an almost, absolute constancy in results.

The process of manufacture, Dr. Prentice explained, was divided into three stages, viz., the mixing of the raw materials, the calcination and the grinding. although there were various methods in use for the first stage of manufacture, according to the physical nature of the raw materials, the one almost universally employed in England was the "wet process," in which chalk and clay were mixed in certain definite chemical proportion and then reduced to a fine state of division in the presence of water. The slurry so obtained was next delivered by mechanical means to 100-feet long rotary furnace, where the evaporation of the water and the calcination took place. It was in , this operation, the lecturer explained, that the chemical` I changes occurred, resulting in the formation of those silicates ond aluminates of calcium, to which Portland cement owed its hardening or" setting" properties, aS they are termed. In the final stake of the process, the semi-vitrified mass produced during the calcination was ground to an impalpable powder. The next part of the lecture dealt with the properties of a good Portland cement and the various tests—chemical and physical--- by which these are assured, this explanation being accompanied by actual demonstration, with the apparatus in daily use. Apropos of the chemical proper- ties, it was pointed out that the grey colour common to all the Portland cements at present in the market, wad4i almost entirely due to the presence of a small percent- age of izon oxide, all the other constituents as he we.. able to show by actual example heing perfectly white. While this did not in the slightest detract from itS strength and suitability for ordinary purposes, it was undeniable that its field of usefulness for artistio exteriors would be immensely enlarged if they were able, as they hoped shortly, to produce on the commer- cial scale a perfectly white cement. In conclusion, Di:. Prentice threw on the screen s numbers of views of buildings and other structures to illustrate the enormous recent developments in the application of cement to constructional and decorative work, especially in combination with steel in the ford of ferro-concrete, one of the most valuable properties of the latter, its wonderful fire-resisting qualities being demonstrated by a number of slides which contrasted its behaviour under fire with that of ordinary materials. A discussion followed, the lecture, Messrs. RonsoNr FISHER, MARSHALL, WRIGHT, and JONES taking part' A hearty vote of thanks to Dr. Prentice, moved 131 Mr. MARSHALL seconded by Mr. COBB for his most interesting and able lecture, was carried with applause*

, Property Agents' Protection Association. A meeting of estate agents of Warrington was held at the offices of Messrs. T. and J. L. Tunstall and Co., Bewsey Chambers, Warrington, on Thursday, when there were present, Mr. C. D. Parkinson (in the chair) and representatives of the following agents :—Messrs. J. Shaw Green and Co., A. H. Clive, T. Cowden, A. W. Howard, J. H. Gittens, J. Titchett and Son, C. Healey/ John Smith, R. Lowe. A. B. Marsh and H. J. Longton• The object of the meeting was to form a Property Agents Protection Association for preventing undesir- able tenants obtaining possession of houses. It was decided at the meeting to form the above Association and Mr. Herbert Johnson of (Messrs. T. and J. L. Association, and Co.) was appointed Secretary, and a committee consisting of the following agente was appointed :—Messrs. A. H. Clive, R. Lowe, John Smith, G. Titchett and C. Healey. The chief object of the Association is to keep a register of the name; of all undesirable tenants, and to take steps to prose- cute persons doing wilful damage to property. A vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman.

Three Years for Housebrqaking. At Liverpool Quarter Sessions on Tuesday, before Sir William Forwood and other magistrates, Hug Beattie (58), fitter, was indicted on three charges of breaking and entering dwelling-houses in Warringtoo and stealing a. number of articles. He pleaded guilty, Mr. Hibbert Ware, who prosecuted, said that in each case the prisoner conducted his operations during the temporary absence of the occupants. He gained entrance by means of a skelton key. On December 26th he entered the house of Edward Powell and stole a silver watch and a gold albert guard and locket. 00 February 21st he broke into the house of Willi Wood and stole a gold chain, brooch, and 15s. io money, and on March 12th he entered the house of Annie Clayton and stole a gold watch and chain and other articles and £3 17s, in money. The prisoner was detected in consequence of giving one of the watches to a man named Bell, who on trying to paw's it, was arrested at St. Helens. Many of the other articles were found under a flag in the prisoners'e house. The Chairman said the prisoner's record wag a very bad one. He got five years' penal servitude, in 1901 for three cases of housebreaking, and had 00 sooner finished that when he received another for seven cases of housebreaking. On that second term he had still one year and 202 days to serve. There was no doubt he was a professional house' breaker, and, the only way to deal with him was to keep him locked up for as long as they reasonabll could, and so prevent him from preying on society, He would have to go to penal servitude for three years, and to serve the balance of the previous sentence.

Dr. Delaney has certified the cause of death. CAMDEN SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY. The anniversary of Camden Sunday school, was cele- biated by special services at St. Paul's Church, Runcorn, on Sunday. Despite the bad weather there was an excellent attendance at the morning service and the church was crowded at the evening service. The preacher morning and evening was the Rev. W. T. Davidson, D.D., ex-president of the Wesleyan Confer- ence and Principal of Richmond College. There were many comments upon the exceptionally good singing of the school children led by St. Paul's Choir. Mr. R. Johnson presided at the organ. The collections for the day amounted to more than £36, beingan advance of £2 on last year's total. On Monday evening Dr. Davison delivered a lecture on "The Bible in the Sunday school of to-day" in St. Paul's Church. Mr. W.Car roll presided ANOTHBR GREAT FIRE AT WIDNES. Another alarming fire occurred about three o'clock on Saturday at the huge bluestone plant of Messrs. Thomas Bolton and Sons, copper manufacturers, behind Waterloo-road, Widnes. The wooden shed, seventy yards square, which contained the patent plant of Mr. Dolphin, used for the extraction of silver from bluestone, was completely wrecked. The flames spread with remarkable rapidity owing to the high wind, but the brigade was successful in saving the adjoining Atlas ' Chemical works belonging to the Atlas Alkali Company. Prior to the arrival of the brigade, the works appliances proved very effective. The whole works were destroyed and damage to the extent of some thousands of pounds was done. About 100 men will be thrown out of employment as the result of the fire. Despite the heavy rain thousands of people watched the progress of the fire. RUNCORN PETTY SESSIONS. At the Runcorn Sessions, on Monday, before Messrs. A. Thomas and T. D. Timmins, William Holt, Lowland- road, was sumEnoned for having kept a dog without a licence. Sergeant Williams said he received a complaint that the dog bit a boy. Defendant was fined in the costs (6s. 6d.)—Edward Wakefield, Loch-street, and Ernest Woodward, Balfour-road, were summoned for a breach of the peace on the 13th inst. Sergeant Salt said he saw defendants fighting in Vicar-street. He separated them. Woodward said Wakefield hit him, but he did not strike back. The bother started about football. Wakefield said he was the better man, and he (Woodward) could have it up or down. In answer to Superintendent Ennion he said he was an Englishman but he did not retaliate. He would sooner run than fight.—The Superintendent: I don't call you an Englishman. Wakefield said he challenged him after some talk about football, and then hit him. He struck back and when the sergeant. came up Woodward gave him in charge. The officer took them both, but Woodward escaped on the way down. Sergeant Frost said Woodward did run away but repented and gave in his name at the Police Station. Defendants were fined in the costs (4s. 6d.) ST. HELENS.

past, because they denied their sacred obligations to the Creator. Is the Creator to be monopolised by the l'ory Party? What arrogant blasphemy 1 When 'these people speak so dogmatically, about religion, do they mean the Christian faith, or their own particular dogmas? Christianity has existed in this country for over 1,800 years without de assistance of the Primrose League, and it will continue to exist, when this institution is dead and forgotten. Fortunately for the audience at this gathering, the newspaper report tells us that an enjoyable entertain- ment followed the speeches. The ventriloquist, the singers, and the pianoforte soloist, provided some com- pensations for the quality of the speeches. The badge of this institution is the beautiful Prim- rose. It is a. pity that so innocent an emblem 4hould be used in such a connection. Poor little primrose, simple flower Thine is a mournful story, What dreadful deed, play bast thou done Thus to be dubbed a Tory. k Once thou wert loved for thine own sake By all who might come near thee, But since a noble Earl " pegged out" A Liberal may not wear thee. But what is that I hear thee say, With mischief in each dimple They claim me for a Tory, sir, Because I am so simple. J. HOWES.
